LADIES' LETTER:

MY DEAR NELLIE, Children's parties are rife at this season. Year in, year out, however, nothing changes their supvernely good taste for white clothes, simply made, hence there is never anything very striking to narrate with regard to their attire. On fancy dress occasions there are opportunities for display. Parents will do well to consider the merits of forester costumes, bird, fruit, and flower dresses, and the less often exploited historical garbs that afford so large a choice. Little children are having their hair dressed now in the old manner; the boys with a manly parting at one side, the girls with their front tresses turned straight back, to leave their pretty brows quite uncovered. Soft white silk frocks inserted with lace and threaded with satin ribbon appertain to the latter, while the lads wear the kilt, the sailor suit, the American cow-boy shirt, with a velvet coat and knicker- bockers, and the regulation Eton costume which always looks well.

Amongst children's dresses may be mentioned a lady's costume of the time of Queen Anne. The skirt should be full, just touching the ground all round, of pale yellow silk or satin over-dress or sacque of blue, yellow, and ivhite-floweted silk open in front and draped back something in the style of a "fish-wife" skirt with bodice of the flowered silk joined to overdress at the back with pointed front laced across a vest of white gauze or lace with yellow cords, neck cut square in front with lace tucker; bell-shaped elbow sleeves with lace frills. Short wide apron of white muslin trimmed with lace and tied with yellow ribbons. Hair dressed high and powdered, with small lace cap and lappets. Another might he of the time of Louis XVI. Dress should be of pink apd mauve brocade over pink satin petticoat, pi &tin cuffs to sleeves, muslin fichu and ruffles, paste buttons and straps of black velvet. Black beaver (or satin) hat, black velvet, paste buttons; white plumes. Powdered hair tied at back with black velvet. Coming to later tap there is the lady's costume for the year 1800. Long dress of soft white muslin should be edged with two narrow frills; full muslin bodice out slightly low in a V-shape with small balloon.puffed short sleeves. Over the bodice there is a deep corselet belt of rich blue velvet fastened with a double row of gold buttons, and blue velvet is run through the lace frills at neck and sleeves. Hair dressed ia close curls with high fancy comb and ostrich feather plume. Very long white glova. should be worn.

There is one kind of costume that I like to see, and that is the pompadour. They are so extremely becoming, and may be carried out in such varied colourings, that they axe always attractive, and almost any dressmaker can make them up. Com- binations of palest green and rose, of pale blue and pink, or of white, yellow, and pink, are mostly employed. A white ground, with the effect of rich Bengaline, has not only white broch4 figures, but large trailing designs in bright pink and autumn-tinted leaves, which would be exquisite over a skirt of rose satin. With this should he worn plenty of old tinted lace, gold and coral, or gold and pearl chains, looped with gem brooches, and the stomacher of pink satin to match the skirt might be crossed by bands of soft lace insertion, or by tiny frills of lace.

You will remember that frieze, with a hairy surface, promised at the beginning of the autumn to make a fresh debut, and is certainly going to be the material above all others for smartness in the new year. It clings, which is desirable, and forms a decided contrast to the smooth-faced cloths of the present moment. When it comes a future will declare itself for cloth hats and gowns en .mite, and the model to be desired will be the turban poking forward in the front, yet not so definitely as to hide the coiffure. Perishable materials are thrown away on chil- dren's frocks, and they are best made of fine cash- mere, light silk or satin, or the stronger makes of muslin or gauze, such as do not tear easily. Coral necklets are being worn again, and the sleevebancls of coral, which all self-respecting babies used to possess, are being utilised for catching up the very short sleeves, which are the fashion for children of from four to seven. The greater simplicity ob- served the better for such youthful wearers, and it is a pity to see the elaborate embroderies on some of this yearcs frocks. Your loving JENNIE.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Queen's arrangements for going abroad this spring, are still in abeyance. They depend upon the health of the Empress Frederick, and also to some extent upon the movements of Mr. Kruger. The ex-president is understood to be going to the French Riviera, and is I even said to select the same town as her Majesty,so tba: he may be able to show how much more popnlar he is in Europe, than Queen Victoria. In any case her Majesty could hardly tolsrate the presence of Mr Kruger, and unless some assurance is to be had on this point, the Queen will no doubt give up the idea of going to Cimiez, and favour San Remo or Be, dighera on the Italian Riviera. This would of course, seriously affect the season at Nice, where Mr Kruger I would probably be less welcome than he ima; nes. Like all people with a grievance, he soon tirA out I, s friends. and be has already worn out his welcome in Holland, where his prolonged stay is not a little embarrassing, ,in view of the forthcoming marriage of Queen Wilbelrnina. The death of the Dowager Lady Churchill recalls the fact that it was a Lady Churchill, nubsequratly Duchess of Marlborough, who was for years the most intimate friend of QU'ell Anne. There is how 4 ver a great differ- ence between that Sovereign and Queen Victoria, as Anne had but little strength of mind, and will, and permitted herself to be governed by her favourite who was perhaps more really Queen of England that Anne herself. Poseibly it was as well for the country that Anne's personal inclinations were thus kept in check. Queen Victoria on the other hand is essentially a woman of character as is shown not moll by her iafluence at Court, but also by the tact ana wisdom which Her Majesty always display's, in apprniating and guiding national sentiment.

The severe illness of the Bishop of London has served to elicit evidence of the extent to which Dr. Creighton has endeared himself by his charity to all sorts and con- ditions of roan. Beth in the diocese of Peterborough, and in that of London he has maintained the most friendly relations with the members of all denomine- Cone, and has afforded practical evidence that he really meant what he said when he spoke to the Congre- gational Union, of the " bond of peace" which he desired to see subsisting. In his efforts to strengthen suet] a bond, he is leading public opinion, and express- ing the feeling of a large number of good people who have had no opportunity of making themselves heard. For that reason, many who are not in communion with the Church of Eughind desire very sincerely his restora- tion to health, and hope that he may not again risk his life by trying to accomplish more work than one man Is capable of compassing. One of the most popular weddings of the season will be that of Major General Pole-Carew, with L%ele Beatrice Butler, even if it is not quite so elaborate as that of the Duke of Westminster, and Miss Cornwallis West. The General belongs to an old Catfish family. He is distantly related to Sir &elver; Buller, and has eerily recently returned from the Cape, after nearly a year's bard fighting with the Boers. The bride who was horn in 1876 is the elder of the two beautiful daughters of the Marquis and Marchionees of Ormonde, and her mother is a daughter of the late Duke of Westminster. Lady Beatrice, and her sister Lady Constance Butler are both heiresses to considerable wealth, but they have no brother, and the heir to the Marquis of Ormoade, is his brother, Lord James Arthur Butler. Neither Lady Beatrice nor Lady Constance Butler care much for town gaieties, preferring infinitely the delights of country life. 'Both girls are devoted to animals, and when at their historic seat—Kilkenny Castle—by far the greater part of their time is spent out of doors. The Castle, which is one of the show places in the country, Is most beautifully situated, and a conspicuous object within a radius of seven miles erf the marble city. One of its Ugreat features is the picture gallery, in which abounds the most valuable of portraits, the majerity dating from the Stuart period, the time of " the groat Duke of Ormende," The Castle also boasts some rare old I tapestry, and a wenderful service of gold plate, which is only used on the stateliest of State occasions. The popular belief that it was a picturesque black- smith who used to preside at the Gretna Green marriages is not borne out by Mr James Murray in his " Life in Scotland a Hundred Years Ago," lie speaks only of an ex-tobacconist in this capacity, who was shunned by all the respectable people of the neighbourhood for his drunken and dissetute habit. This was perhaps rather in keeping than otherwise with the laxity of the whole formality. At one time as many as three or four hun- dred marriages were performed dierin,g the year by the soi disant parsons of Gretna Green, but they were mostly of the lowest class of people from England. It was only the few who belonged to the higher ranks, and almost the last of these scandals, was the clandestine marriage of Lady Adele Villiers, a daughter of a former Earl of Jersey, with Captain Ibbetson. This event led to strong representations being made in influential quarters, and there is no doubt that it hastened the alteration of the law in Scotland. which virtu.lly abolished the privileges of Gretna Green, after 1856. The explanation why the wedding ring is worn on the third finger of the left band, has never bean satisfactor- ily determined. All that learned,writers are agreed upon is, that a gift of a ring was held by the most ancient peoples of the earth, to be a necessary part of the marriage ceremony. Some authorities say that the ring is worn on the left hand to show woman's subjection, or second place in the human family. Another theory is that the left hand was chosen because it is the least employed of the two, and that, for much the same reason the third finger was selected, because it is the least used, and the beet protected of any. Others speak of an old belief in a sempathetio connection with par- ticular nerves or arteries of the third finger, with the heart.but though this may have had something to do with establishing the custom, it is the merest fancy from a physinal point of view. Upon the whole, there seems most to be said for the theory that the adoption of the third, or next least finger, was a matter of convenience, or of preserving the ring as much as possible from wear. The fashionable bride now dons her veitrin two sec• tionr, the corner which falls over the face being separate. After the ceremony this corner is unpinned - a much more becoming and comfortable arrangement than throming it hack, a position which it invariably refuses to keep, however skilfully coaxed. Heavy lace veils are quite out of vogue, tulle being the favourite wear, finished at the edge with a tiny chiff sn ruche. There is quite a mania at the moment for bead neck- laces, and much rummaging in old chests, and trinket boxes, is in progress. The great thing is • careful selec-, tion of beade,otherwise the effect makes for tawdrios es Some very good venetian species, probably left o v from the msthetic days, are beieg worn by up-te-1 women, and a novel idea is not to make the chain into a circle, but finish each end with a tassel of the heads. This chain to be correct, should pass twice round the neck, the ends hanging down each aide on the bodice, The storm collar Is now rarely seen, and consequently the vast crop of sore throats and colds at one time usual at this season, is in great measure conspicuous by its absence. Tippets or eollarettes of fur are the wear, but are rarely fastened up, nearly all opening at the throat te show a little lace in jabot form. Even the favourite silk ruffle edged with fur is worn well on the shoulders, a net only fascinating, but healthful arrangement. The more one coddles the throat, the greater chance one stands of taking a chill. The great thing is to hit the happy medium and then keep to it. " MADGE."

TENNYSON AND THE "OORNHILL"

It occurred to ma that if I could secure Tennyson as a regular contributor to the new magazine he would prove a great attraction. Elis " Idylls of the King" had net long appeared, and I thought I would ask him to Write for us another set of " Idylls.' Tennyson was then on a visit to Mrs. Cameron on Putriev Heath. and ( wrote to ask if I might call upon him on matter of business. We made an appointment, and during our interview I offered to pay him five thousand guineas for as many lines as were contained in the " Idylls of the King "(in fact, for 4,750 lines), on condition that the poems should be printed in the "Cornhill Magazi-le" and ' that I should publish them for three years afterwards. That offer was really a " record "as far as the market rates of poetry up to that time were concerned. When compared with anything. Tennyson had yet received for his poems it might fairly be described as extravagant. Tennyson listened to my Proposal with entire calmness. Be asked me to smoke with him and chatted plesaantly ' • but rave me no idea as to whether my offer was acceptable • Mrs Tennyson presently came into the room. and Tennyson, address- ing her. said : "My deer, we are much richer than we thought we were. Mr. Smith has just offered MP fire thousand guineas for a book the faza of the ' And" he continusd, tt if Mr. Smith offers five thousand. of ClUrlie the book is worth ten ! " A remark at which we all laughed. Nothing came of this proposal, which I had no temptation to renew after the rau;d SUCCe3% achieved by the magazine. But Thackeray obtained from Tennyson his fine poem "Tithonus " for the second number.— From " Our Birth and Parentage," in the " Cornhill Magazine" for January.

Volunteer Ball.

The New Year's Eve ball in connection with the I local Volunteer Battalion was held in the Drill Ball, on Monday, The arrangements were satisfactorily carried out by the mess president and committee, and the duties of M.C. were discharged by Colour- Sergeant Spencer, assisted by Sergeant-Trumpeter Plinston. Dancing was commenced about 8.30, and continued until after 2 o'clock the following morning, nearly 200 persons being present. The music was supplied by the band of the cori-P,under the conductorship of Bandmaster Richardson. The caterißg was in the hands of Mr R Anderson, Cross Keys Inn, Buttermarket-street.

HARRIS'S, 28-30, BERRY SYREET, LIVERPOOL.

THE DUTIES OF THE "MILITARY KNIGHTS."

" Among the many objects of historical interest which present themselves; to the visitor at one of the beautiful morning cervices held daily at St. George's Chapel, Windsor." writes F Kliekrnatin in the December number of the WINDSOR MAGAZINR, "the appearance of a num- ber of notilr-,oolsing old gentlemen of decidedly military hearing, whose bright scarlet uniforms are so mush in keeping with the splendour of the choir, cannot but , excite interest as to their origin and history. These are the Military Kr.ighte of Windsor. The duties of these veterans are by no meaai arduous, and considerable relaxatioa has been accorded to some of the mos; rigorous of farmer regulations. An uefailing daily attendance at Divine Service in St. George's Chapel is no longer compnlsory—indeed Iller Majesty has sanctioned ! this being dispensed with entirely where the Knights are Roman Catholics ; nevertheless, the members of the Order are supposed to be in their Stalls as regularly as possible, and this should not be a difficult matter to men whose time is not burdened with much else in the way of official duties. They are also required to attend at any installation that may be held in the Chapel, such as those of the Knight of the Garter, or of their own Order. They are likewise included in ISMS of the Court cere- monials, and form a picturesque adjunct at Royal recep- tions. Fun ' erals either those of Royal personages,buried in the historic Chapel, or those of the members of their fraternity, gain much in magnificence by the inclusion of these old officers in their gorgeous apparel, who always form part of the procession. The scarlet full dress is worn on State occasions, Sundays, and festivals ; the undress uniform, with the blue frock coat, is worn on Litany days, while mufti is permitted for evensong. The undress uniform is provided by the Knights out of their ewn revenues, and costs them about £l5 for each suit. This alone is somewhat a strain on their modest pension of £49 pr annum. They are allowed three months' leave of absencs each year—not a bad scale of holidays, one would imagine! Toe main bulk of the money paid to the Knights is derived from ths manor of Carbrook, Norfolk, on which a charge of £230 is annually levied, and out of which £4B is deduoted as 'Lod Tax '—a perceptible diministion on so small a sum! When it is taken into consideration that the Knights have to pro- vide themselves with light, fuel, medical attendance, and such like items to run a way with money, it will be seen that, much as the vacancies are essveted, they are of but small m Aerial benefit to the Knights themselves.

RUBY LOVEDAY.

BY SILAS K. HOCKING,

Author of "The Strange Adventures of Israel Pendray," "The Day of Recompense," "God's Outcast," "In Spite of Fate," "Her Benny, " Arc.

CHAPTER I.

===== 0002601_19010104_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
FAMILY PRIDE.

"It is not empty vanity - To prize the name you bear," "I hate the whole business; and I kno-r I shall hate the people, with their stuck-up London ways." "I don't know why you should, Justin," his mother answered, in hurt tones; "it seems to me you ought to be very thankful." "I know I ought," was the impatient reply; "that is the annoying part of it. The money will be welcome enough, Heaven knows; but think of the annoyance, the inconvenience, the—the-----" "I knew what you would say, Justin," his mother interposed, mildly; "but pride is poor fuel to boil the crock with. Besides, if anyone suffers any inconvenience, it will not be you. Dorothy and I will have to bear the brunt of that." "Of course you will," he answered, in milder tones. "But don't you see that that doesn't mend matters in the least? I would a hundred times rather slave myself than see you slaving." "We are not going to kill ourselves, Justin. You need not fear that. Besides, it is only a matter of two months, and we shall drop back into the old ways again." "I'm not so sure of that," he answered, slowly. "These innovations and disturbances never leave things exactly as they were before. Next year, very likely you'll want to let the rooms again. Having made a beginning, it will be easy to go on. And then—" "Yes, and then, Justin?" she questioned, sharply. "There's nothing dishonourable that I know of in letting part of your house during the summer, especially when seasons are bad and money is scarce. Besides, the Lovedays will not be like ordinary lodgers. I shall look upon them in the light of paying guests. You know what Cousin Phil says about them." "I do. I wish he had said less. These London Nabobs expect to be waited upon hand and foot. Paying guests, indeed! They'll regard you as a paid servant." "You know nothing about it, Justin. You might at least wait until they arrive. It doesn't follow because they are rich that they won't know how to behave." "I know more than you think," he answered, shortly. "When Phil was down at Easter, we had low, talks together, and he told me how those London swells live. Look at Phil himself, and he wasn't to the manner born, and yet it's easy to see what he thinks of us now." "If Phil had been to the manner born, as you call it,. he would not put on so many airs and graces," was the quick reply. "That's the worst of those people who get on so fast. For a genuine snob, commend me to someone who was dug out of the gutter." Justin laughed and shrugged his shoulders. "That's a bit hard on your own relatives, isn't it ? " he questioned. "Relatives or no, there's no denying that Phil was dragged up in a rough-and-ready fashion. That wasn't poor Mary's fault, for she died when he was a baby. We none of us thought anything of Passmore, and were all sorry when she married him." "He managed to give Phil a good start, any- how." "He managed to do nothing of the sort. lie died heavily in debt, as everybody knows. Phil has been fortunate, though I don't deny he's clever as well." "Of course he's clever. Why, he's getting a thousand a year at the present time, if he's getting a penny, and here am I passing rich on a pound a week." "My dear boy, a man may be just as happy on a pound a week as on twenty pounds a week." "Yes, I've heard that before," was the reply. "Nevertheless, I would like to try the twenty pounds a week just by way of experiment. I con- fess I'm getting sick of grubbing along in this humdrum way fi•orn year to year, especially when we've to take in lodgers to help things out. "Nobody said we were compelled to take in lodgers, Justin. Your father objected to it at first. But you know the farm is doing badly this season, and Dorothy and I thought we might as well make a few pounds when we had the chance. Three pounds a week for eight weeks running is not to be sneered at." "I know that very well, mother; but don't you see how humiliating it is to father and to me that we are not able to earn enough between us to keep things going as we would like. Ah, if I were only earning a quarter of what Phil gets, there would be no talk of taking in lodgers." "Your father says nothing about humiliation. He seems to have less pride than his son. He thinks if you earn what you earn honestly, nothing else is worth troubling about." "Between pride and a reasonable self-respect," Justin said, shortly, "there is a difference. I own I dislike the idea of letting apartments, for we have carried our heads pretty high in the past. And I dislike especially letting them to people who are used to something so totally different. I can fancy how Miss Loveday will turn up her little nose with scorn at our primitive ways, and old-fashioned furniture, and country methods of doing things. I hope, at any rate, she will let off none of her criticisms in my hearing." "You needn't see her that I know of, Justin, or any of the others. They will have the front part of the house, and we shall live in the back. The front door and the front stairs' will be for their exclusive use while they are here, so you needn't

worry." "I sincerely hope I shall not see them," was the hasty reply. "I know if I do, I shall have a difficulty in being civil." "I think you are very unreasonable, Justin. You have taken a prejudice against people you have never seen, and are prepared to listen to nothing in

_ their defence." "I expect lam a bit unreasonable," he said, after a pause, rising slowly to his feet. "Anyhow, I will make a point of keeping out of their way," and he turned and walked out of the room. In the open air he felt in a less captious frame of mind. It was a fresh, breezy evening in late June. The scent of newly-cut hay filled the atmosphere. The old orchard that stretched away from the back of the house to the foot of the slope seemed alive with twittering birds, and over all lay the soft glamour of the evening sunshine. For awhile he loitered in the orchard, and interested himself in examining the young apples, some of which were already beginning to shew a touch of red on their cheeks. Then he climbed over a stile at the far end, and began to ascend the slope towards a plantation of firs that crowned the summit, and sheltered the old farm-house from the north-westerly gales. At the edge of the plantation he paused and turned round, and, leaning his shoulder against a rail fence, surveyed with a little thrill of pride the quiet pastoral scene that lay at his feet. Endilloe had been the home of the Pentyres for half-a-dozen generations at least, and because the house, together with the garden, orchard, and several meadows was freehold, they had been people of some dis- tinction in the neighbourhood, for owners of free- hold Were few and far between. There was- a tradition that an earlier Pentyre owned a Hundred acres of land besides, contiguous to the house and garden, but that a great county magnate, who owned nearly the whole of the parish of St. lago, with a number of other parishes, dis- puted his title, and succeeded at length in wresting the farm from him, and adding it to his own pos-

sessions. Anyhow the hundred acres of land that sur- rounded Endilloe were rented to-day, and heavily rented, too. And it was this that kept the Pentyres on the edge of respectable poverty and sent Justin into the neighbouring town of Trelford to earn the munificent sum of fifty-two pounds ten shillings per annum as principal salesman at a seed, manure, and agricultural implement store. Justin was a high-spirited young fellow, having a full share of pride and considerable faith in his own abilities if fortune would only give him a chance of exercising them. At present he believed he was buried alive. There was no scope for any kind of ability at Trelford. The little town was asleep six days out of seven. It was only on market days that it ahewed any sign of being awake. Then farmers drove in from all the country round to make their purchases and sell their farm produce. Then their wives and daughters sat in rows in the tiny market-house with baskets of butter and eggs on their knees. Then boys and maidens who had half-

noliday or were -out or work walked Into the town to enjoy themselves. Then the shopkeepers pat on their best clothes and smiled benignly on all their customers. Then the store—in which Justin held so conspicuous a place—put oil its most festive look, and everyone appeared to be in the greatest hurry, whether customers were present or absent—for it was a tradition among the shop. keepers of Trelford that the best way of attracting customers was to appear to be overwhelmingly busy. Moreover, it was a tradition founded on experience. Customers were known to fight shy of shops where nothing appeared to be "going on," and it was even hinted that certain tradesmen arranged for their friends and domestics even to stand round in their shops in slack times so as to give an appearance of great commercial activity. Justin—though he loathed the weekly hypocrisy, and longed to get away from the narrow, niggling life to which he was doomed—was quite an expert in giving an appearance of activity even in the slackest hours of that weekly market-day. In fact, he had earned the reputation of being the smartest salesman in Trelford ; and so much did his employers value his services that they had, unsoli- cited, raised his salary from fifty pounds a year to fifty-two pounds ten. But though he had practically reached the top of the tree he was very far from being satisfied. True, it was better than working a rack-rented farm on insufficient capital, as his father and grand- father had done before him. He was thankful that he had broken the succes- sion of farmers. If the hundred acres his father farmed had been freehold, as it was originally, he would gladly have stepped into his father's shoes and handed on the succession. But to toil year in and year out for the enrichment of a man who was rich to rottenness already; to see all the value of improvements claimed by the owner, who never lifted a finger to improve his own property—that he would never consent to. He told his father, the day after he left school, that he would go to sea, or enlist as a soldier, or work underground in the mines; but a farmer he never would be, and that nothing on earth should compel him. He quite expected that this outburst would be met by strong coercive measures. But to his sur- prise his father smiled sadly, and said: "Very well, Justin, I shall not force you into it." "Then you are not angry with me?" "No, my lad. If you can find an easier way through the world find it by all means. There's no pleasure in farming in these days." "Is there ever any pleasure in it ?" the boy asked.

"There might be. I can conceive of nothing more delightful under some circumstances. To watch the seeds shoot, and the trees bud, and the corn ripen; to listen to the cackling of the poultry and the bleating of the lambs; to stand in the soft /rummer rain when the earth is dry and watch it drinking up the moisture; to lie awake of summer nights and hearken to the corncrake in the far-off fields. Ah, my boy, what can be finer than that. But when you remember that the fruit of your toil is not yours, that the lambs, and the corn, and the fruit have all to be sold to pay a rent that the land will not bear; that you plod, and plan, and pinch yourself from January to Christmas to find yourself at the end of the year poorer than at the beginning; that if you make an improvement or find a more remunerative market for your produce your rent is raised at the next letting day—well, under circumstances like these there is no joy in it. The people who do nothing and who, in the sight of Heaven, have no more right to the land than you have, reap all the fruit of your toil, and you grub your way to the grave in weariness and despair." Justin looked up at his father in wonder, lie had never heard him make so long a speech before, nor one that was so tinged with bitterness, for John Pentyre was a meek and gentle-spirited man, who was rarely heard to complain, and who accepted his lot with patient resignation. "Then you will let me try to get my living in some other way ? " the boy asked at length. "Yes, my lad, try your best. I think I can trust you not to do anything foolish." So on the following day the lad went to Trelford and engaged himself as an errand-boy, and so had worked himself up to his present position. Justin thought of all this as he leant against the fence and looked down upon the quiet home- stead nestling among the fruit trees at his feet. Yet there was no feeling of exultation in his heart. Part of his salary also went to pay the rent, and now, as a last humiliation, lodgers were to be taken, and the best rooms in the house were to be handed over to strangers.

CHAPTER 11. A WAGER. "The life of every man is a diary In which he means to write one story And writes another."

A few evenings later, on his return from Trelford, his sister Dorothy met him at the garden gate. "You had better come round to the back door, Justin," she said, in a half-whisper. "Our visitors have arrived." "Oh, indeed " Then he paused suddenly. Other words were on the tip of his tongqe, but second thoughts came to his rescue. Nothing was to be gained by making himself disagreeable, especially as upon Dorothy nod his mother would fall the bulk of the worry mid inconvenience. "I know you don't like it," Dorothy went on. "You think it is humiliating; but I don't see why. Everybody does it nowadays. You see, Cornwall is becoming quite popular, and I don't see why we should not take advantage of the vogue and make hay while the sun shines." - "I don't like to see you turned into a waitress, Dorothy," he said, with a smile, "but if you don't mind, it is not for me to complain." "Oh, I don't mind in the least," she said, brightly; "indeed, I think I shall enjoy it for a change. But, do you know, I was terribly nervous before they came." "And now ?" he questioned. "Why, you will scarcely believe it, but I feel as though I had known them a low".' time already." "I suppose they have patronised you and called you by your Christian name? Just like those purse-proud nobodies with their familiar ways." "Now you are making yourself nasty, Justin, and assuming what isn't true. Nobody could have been nicer, and they have treated me with every respect. Why will you be so prejudiced against people that you know nothing about?" "I don't know, Dorothy; it is very foolish of me, no doubt. But is supper ready, for I am terribly hungry in spite of the heat ?" "It's ready and waiting. But don't be long over it, for I want you to take me for a walk. I've been in the house the whole of the day, and, besides, I have a lot to tell you." "All right. You remain in the orchard till I come out," and he disappeared suddenly into the house. - .

Dorothy walked up and down under the trees swinging her straw hat in her hand. The sun was setting behind the plantation, and decorating the uppermost boughs with long streaks of vermilion and gold. Nearly all the fields lay in shadow, and in the orchard a cool wind stirred and awoke low whispers among the leaves. It was an ideal summer evening—cool and fragrant after the sultry heat of a cloudless day. Justin took full time over his supper, bu. Dorothy shewed no impatience. It was just delightful to loiter under the trees and listen to the sparrows chirping in all directions and feel the soft breath of the wind stirring the straying locks of soft brown hair that lay so lightly upon her *ow. Delightful, too, to dream her maiden dreams and weave all manner of pleasant fancies, and see her castles rising in the air great, and noble, and stroll.. She was of a hOpeful temperament, for in reality, there was not much prospect of any of her dreams being realised. But it was not her nature to argue out such questions. She was young and a woman, and it is of the very genius of youth to fling rainbow arches across impassable ravines, and of the indestructib'e nature of a woman to hope when all ground of hope has been taken away. Dorothy had her silent hours of sorrow, when her lips trembled in spite of herself and the tears were abundant upon her cheeks. But she never despaired. Her hope was as constant as the day- light, and what to others were proven facts were to her but idle dreams, which the unfolding of some future day would scatter to the winds. Justin came out at length, and without a word they walked away together. Dorothy took his arm 'as they climbed the slope. But they did not speak until they got into the heart of the plantation. By this time the sun had disappeared, but the sky was still aflame with his light, and the wind was humming merrily in the tree tops. A few minutes' brisk walking and they came out on the other side of the plantation, and in a moment the whcle scene was changed. Right and left stretched a heathery common, destitute of tree or shrub, and in front lay the gnat ocean still and almost silent, like a monster fast asleep.

"true sea scareety moans to-nignt," .uorotnr said, as she stood on the brink of the cliff and looked down. "Deark,-n, Justin, did you hear that deep note, or, rather, did you feel it, for it is almost too deep to be heard Y" "Oki Neptune is dreaming," he said, with a male. "Look out yonder by the headland and sea how hie bosom heaves. After all the mischief he has wrought, it is no wonder that he sleeps uneasily." "And yet we love the great restless creature," Dorothy answered, with her eyes still fixed on the rising tide below. Then she turned suddenly. "It's too much trouble to go down on the rocks," she said. "Let's sit here on the heather." "It would be too much trouble to climb up again," he answered, following her. "Besides, I thought you had a lot to tell me ?" "So I have when we can find a comfortable seat. Ah, here is an arm-chair, cushioned and com- plete," and she sat down suddenly in a bank of heather. In 'a few moments he was seated by her side, and they were looking off upon the trackless ocean that was growing solemn and myterious under the gathering shadows. "Did it ever occur to you, Dorothy," he said, "that modern discovery has robbed the world of nearly all its romance ? ' "No," she answered, demurely. think I know exactly what you mean." "I mean that as far as the world itself is con- cerned there is little or nothing more to find out. Think of how this great ocean would appeal to our forefathers. It was a mystery unmeasured and unexplored to them. They would stand here ott these very cliffs and wonder what lay out there beyond the sunset—wonder if there were other lands or other worlds, or if the sea stretched away for ever. What scope there was for the romancers of those days, what a field for the imagination. But there's no room for romance any more. Those shadows out there on the horizon don't impress me with any sense of mystery. I know the size of the ocean and the shape of it. Ido not wonder what lies beyond. I know America is out there on the slope of tlie circle. We've measured up all the oceans and explored all the continents, and made s chart of the bottom of the sea, and there's nothing left to wonder about or excite the imagination." "Oh, that's all nonsense, Justin," she said, in her quiet way. "You have not seen the world when you've looked at a map, nor all that is in it because you have been to Wombwell's wild beast show. I really think it is time you had a change

"And I don't

of air." "I think so myself," he answered, with a laugh, "but, unfortunately, there's no such luck. A. pound a week won't run to such luxuries." "No, I suppose not. But let us not get on to that subject, please. I want to talk to you about something much more pleasant." "That's good of you. I'm all attention." "Do you remember saying last week that you would wager your Sunday hat Miss Loveday was a vinegary- female of uncertain age, and dowered with a long catalogue of ailments ?" "Did I say so much ?" "You did. As a matter of fact you said a great deal more, but I've forgotten the rest; not that that matters, for it was not worth remembering." "No ? Then you've done wisely, my sweet sister, in forgetting." "I know that very well. But what I want to Impress upon you is, the foolishness of prophesying until you know." "May I ask if that remark is original'?" he inquired, with mock seriousness. "No, you may not. You want to turn me off on a side issue again, but I'm not going to let you. I expect you to bring me a new hat from Trelford to-morrow, and don't let it be a shabby one, either." "Oh, my l" ho said, with a laugh, "this is getting serious. But let me see where we are. You say that I wagered my Sunday hat that Miss Love- day was an elderly spinster." "Exactly, and you've lost." "But as far as 1 remember you did not accept the wager." "And if I didn't that's nothing to do with it. You said what wasn't true, and so you've lost." "In that case, then, I shall have to give you my Sunday hat." "Your Sunday hat, indeed," and she laughed a low, silvery laugh. "But you are just like a man, Justin, you seem unable to see a plain issue whem it is placed before you." "I'm afraid that is true," he answered, trying to look grave. "But, admitting your distinctly feminine argument to be conclusive up to the present point, I fail to see, even now, how you have established your claim." "Well I never. How blind you must be." "Granted that that is true," he answered, hi the same bantering tone, "still, you have given me no proof that what I said was not correct." "No proof. Haven't I told you that she is the very opposite?" "Your testimony is valuable, no doubt, my dear sister; still, it is not proof." "Oh, isn't it ?" was the ready answer. "Do you think I've no eyes, or that I don't know how to use them. I tell you, Ruby Loveday is the - prettiest creature you ever saw, and she isn't more than nineteen if she's a day." "And is this the news you were so eager to communicate?" he questioned. "I wanted to prove to you how stupid and preju-. diced you have been. From the very first you made up your mind that the Lovedays would be disagreeable people. Well, they are nothing of the sort, they are the-very opposite, in fact, and as for Ruby—well, all I can say is you had better remain out of her way if you wish to keep heart.* I whole." "I intend to keep out of their way," he answered, "but not for any such reason as you suggest. To begin with, I don't want to be patronised or asked to run errands, and in the next place I have no . interest in people whom I have never seen, and when they leave here we shall never see again." Dorothy rose to her feet and laughed. "Look here, Justin," she said, "I'll wager that new hat you owe me that before a month is over you'll be , more interested in the Lovedays than any of us." "Indeed. But I thought you advised me just now not to prophesy?" "So I did. And I tell you again you should never do so, for you aro always miles and miles out when you try." "Don't you think you would be safe in follow-. ing your own advice ? ' "Not in this case, for I know what I am talking about. What lam afraid of is that you will fall head over heels in love, and then the last state of that man will be worse than the first." "It would be very much worse," he answered, with a smile. "But you needn't fear, Dorothy. If I ever fall in love it will not be with a City miss. I shall need a wife, if 'ever I marry, who understands housekeeping, and who knows the value of a shilling." "Well, then, you had better keep out of Ruby Loveday's way, for she has the sweetest and the most bewitching eyes you ever saw. I tell you I'm lialf in love with her myself; and you know girls don't think much of girls as a rule. But Ruby Loveday is just lovely, she is indeed. She le as dainty as a spring flower, and as graceful as a fawn; not that I know what a fawn is, but it's the proper kind of simile I know." "Yes, go on," he said, with another laugh, "the simile is quite appropriate. What other good qualities sloes she possess ?" "Oh, she's sweet tempered, chi) is, and good and kind. I'm soy fail in love with her, Justin." "I hope not, Dorothy. dianoec are I shall not see her they', • t, .:.0 Miuch danger in that directioi "Well, your only 11,.0e is ti beep out of the way. But who comes her, I wonder ?" and she stepped aside and peered.int o the shadowy planta- tion. "It's the Loveclays, as I'm alive," she whispered. "They've come out to explore the neighbourhood and catch a glimpse of the sea before it grows quite dark. There's no escape for you, Justin, so steel your heart and do the agreeable at the same time." (To be continued.)

LIFE A BURDEN AT EIGHTEEN. (See portrait above.)

A Norwich Mercury reporter had a chat the other day with Mrs. Easton, landlady of the Ratcatchers' Inn, at the little village of Cawston, near Norwich, when she told the reporter the whole story of the remarkable recovery of her daughter Harriet. EIGHTEEN YEARS OF AGE. Mrs. Easton said :—" Harriet was first taken with fainting fits and sickness. Her weakness grew on her gradually. At last she could not get upstairs without losing her breath, and as for sweeping a room, she was no more fit for the work than a child. Her heart, too, was so bad that we could hear it beating right across the room. If she tried to walk into the village, she was tired before she got there. I had doctors in, who said she was suffering from anemia. She took to her bed for several weelis, but seemed very little better when she managed to get up again. "It was just then that we read a little book telling us about Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and the wonderful cures they had effected. We decided to try a box, and she had not taken the pills more than two days before we noticed a decided change for the better. She was not so low-spirited as she had been: the heart's action no longer distressed her and her breathing improved. But for Dr. Williams' Pink Pills I feel sure she would not have been here now." Judging by the bonny looks of the daughter, who sat close by, substantiating her mother's story, gooNhealth was regained rapidly. Mrs. Easton added: "Some days she would eat nothing; now she has a really good appetite. She used to make me quite nervous, but Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have made a woman of her." A WARNING,

'THIS WONDERFUL CEN- TURY.'

INTERESTING REVIEW BY THE REV. J. YONGE.

Wycl:ff Congregational Church, Warrington, on Sunday ,woning. the Rev. Junta Yongo selected as a fitting uhc " Thli Wonderful Ceutury." "He bath made We wonderful works to be remembered" (Psalm 111 Verse 4 ) It would be a misuse of that unique opportunity, he sail if our time were occupied with a mere epitome of event, or achievement, or development, or progress as such. Therefore, in attempting what in the brief time now at disposal. seems beset with insuperable difficulty, to give compressed account of the 19th Century, in some of its principal events and characteristics, their endea- vour must be to think of the wonderful period as a manifold, continual revelation of Divine thought and op ration. SERIOUS EFFECTS OF A SHOCK IN OLD AGE.

(See portrait above.)

Mr. John Bates, of Home Cottage, Mill-lane, Sydenharn, London, S.E., told the story of his great misfortune to a representative of the Crystal Palace Times, recently. He said :--" One night in October, 5898, I was walking home through Wells-road when I was knocked down by a horse and cart, and nearly stunned. I was cut over the eye, and, besides being badly shaken, was severely bruised all over my body. I was removed home, put to bed, where I lay for three weeks, and for another six weeks I felt very bad indeed. Such an accident for a man of my years—

I AN NOW SEVENTY-FOUR —was rather serious, and I sustained a shock which affected me to such an extent that I suffered badly from loss of memory. I was, of course, in the hands of a doctor, yet I did not seem to recover. Then my right arm and leg became affected, and I could not use eiL'ier of them much. I had to give up my business, and for a con.iderable time could only hobble about. " .7ut I'm much better now. I found after the doctor left me that my health was far from satisfactory; I could not seem to get well at all. Then my son suggested that I should try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. We had read a number of cases where cures had been brought about in people suffering from various ailments, and my son brought me a box of the pills. I commenced to take them, carefully following the directions, and quickly felt the difference: I was very much better by the time the box was exhausted. I recommend Dr. Williams' Pink Pills whenever I have an opportunity, for I am a firm believer in them after the change one box v(orked in me."

You take your letter to the post, or you receive your letter fro= the postman and you deem it the very commonest ad , most ordinary incident of daily life. You expect to send or receive letters to or from the ends of the earth in brief spaces, and with the utmost regularity and for the smallest cost. If you want to be still more expeditious in communication than the express train or the mail steamer by employing the telegraph, you reach your friend in the distant town or at the antipodos at lightn- ing speed. Or you may even actually talk with your friend in London or Edinburgh or Paris by using the telephone, by which he can hear you speaking here,and you can listen to him speaking thorn. THE 4 RIGHTS OF WOM&NHOOD.

'THE SCANDALS OF THE 19TH CENTURY

considerable as to produce for a moment an apathy of self-satisfaction." 'Maio words of the eminent, although at present somewhat indefinable statesman,serve to point out that " Freedom's battle oft begun" will have in many matters to be again begun in the New Centary. There are many tyrannies still rife : many inequalities yet extant : many social conditions, even in free coun- tries like our own, which .call for re-adjustment, and In some cases utter destruction. But the general drift of this age has been towards liberty. In that tendency, shall we not gratefully recognise the uplifting and redeeming power acid grace of God 10

Wo.en, as well as men, should rejoice to recognise how much the rentury has done towards the advance- ment of the rights of womanhood. It may not be the , case that the curious eccentricity, known as the " new woman," commends itself to tha intelligent and the seemly of either sex. That woman should be elevated to her rightful equality with man does not necessarily or properly involve that she should either usurp his style, or seek to occupy his place. But that woman should have personal rights of property equally with man, and personal political rights, has becom3 generally recognised and is being gradually realised. There are splendid social services that woman Can render from which old prejudices, and even old laws, have long debarred her. These have been steadily giving way, and woman can now share in the councils of poor law unions, and school boards, in both of which realms she can be of invalu- able service. She will preseatly fled access to county councils, and if county, why not town ? There are social laws which can only be best administered, or adjusted, or created by the help of women. The 19th Century in this kingdom has arrived at the recognition of this, and the 20th will no doubt complete the realisa- tion. The youneer woman of to-day will do well to have an eye to all kinds of social service. There are countless sisterly works that they can do, loving minis- tries and services of practical common womanly sense, by which they can bless human life. There can be little doubt that the large uplift and development of the Int and service of woman may be considerably due to the influence and example of that great and good woman' who for the greater part of this century has occupied the throne of this realm. POPULAR EDUCATION.

Think yet again of the vast development this Ago has seen in Popular Education. When, in the course of the preceding century, Raikes became the founder of Sunday Schools, be was largely moved to it by the double ignorance of the children, their ignorance of the G and their ignorance of the commonest elements of knowledge. It has remained for this Century to per- ceive that Elementary Elucation is the birthright of every child : that it is National Duty to impart it, and to pay for it. The Progress of this National Work has been beset and is still beset with manifold difficulties, mainlylarising from ecclesiastical assumptions and relig- ious intolerance. But no one can doubt that the Great Eiucational, Revolution inaugurated in 1870 by the late W E Forster, has produced untold and incalculable benefit to this nation at large. The 20th century, it is to be confidently expected, will not be many years old ere a complete scheme of National Education shall be perfected and,established, and the still existing impedi- ments of denominational interests and bigotry will be swept aside. THE CONDITION OF THE 43EOPLE. . .

Now, it might, perhaps, have been thought that a century which has introduced so many popular reforms, —by which the government of the country has become vested in the hands of the people,—would have seen larger improvement in the condition of the people. When in the middle of the century, the governing power was entrusted to the masses, as distinguished from the classes, it was fully expected that the masses would have effected some far reaching changes in their social state. That has not been done at any rate to the extent we would hava wished. Still there exist conditions of human life, in the larger towns and cities especially, which are a disgrace to civilization, a satire noon Christianity, and a menace to the public weal. Still 'it continues to be possible,—almost it sometimes seems even more possible than ever,—for bloated wealth to luxuriate at the West End, while gauntest and most hideous poverty shivers and starves at the East End.

in this country are the millionaires and the starving! And yet,it must not be forgotten what vast amelioration of the state of the destitute poor has been effected by Poor Law Reform, so largely helped in their days by the pens of Thomas Hood and Charles Dickens. The work- houses of to-day are palaces, and the Workhouse Infirmaries Royal Hospitals compared with similar institutions, 50 or 100 years ago. Still the inevitable, inalienable stigma attaches to these last refuges of tha poor, but anyway they are humanely managed upon the truest principles of sanitatioo, and with:every neces- sary provision of food, warmth and comfort. All this has come about under the practical influence of a phil- anthropic humanity, whose spirit is of God. The preacher proceeded to show that England of to-Jay is a better place to live in than the England of a century ago. After a reference to the revival of militarism brought about by the unhappy war in South Africa, " Perhaps the moment has hardly come," the preacher continued, " when this nation of ours will listen with tolerant attention to councils of toleration,but the coming of that moment may well be the burden of every Christian's prayer. Reference was also made to the fact that the century had been a century of missions:

RIDAY, JANUARY 4, 1901

nowever, Is proverni:l4ny ti,rn to conquer, ana partly for that rea.on and partly on account of a certain •11 i. not unfreouently the outcome of a determination to succeed in one's attempts to promote what one calls, but knows very well it is not, the ultimate good of a second person, he behaved like a bear for the remainder of the day. Nothing pleased him, and it was not until he had finished his third bottle and had been carried unconscious upstairs to bed by his servants that the house was at peace. As in duty bound his lordship had shared his potations, but he had graduated in a good school, and he owned a head that was not to be affected by even three bottles of a Hampshire squire's port Having seen his future father-in-law removed he made his way to the drawing-room. His dissipated countenance was a little flushed, and when he spoke his voice possessed a thickness which was not there at other times, but that was all. To his delight he found the Squire's daughter alone. She had been seated at her spinet, but on seeing him she rose and endeavoured to retreat. There was a look in her face that at any other time would have warned him that she was not in a humour to be trifled with. In his present condition, however, the fact that she was antagonistic to him gave additional zest to his love-making.

"Fortune- favours me at last," he cried, as he made his way towards her. "This is the oppor- tunity I have long been waiting for." • • - - - -

- - As he said this he endeavoured to take her in his arms. She recoiled from him in terror, but he was not to be denied. At last he had her in his grasp. lie pulled her towards hill until his flushed, excited face was only a few inches from her own.

-- --- At that moment the window was thrown up, and a man appeared from the dark outside. He was young, handsome and audacious, and he took in the situation at a glance. His lordship had scarcely time to note the presence of the new-comer before he found himself seized violently by the collar and lying on his back upon the floor. It was so sudden that he had no time to protect himself or to call for assistance. "Lie there, you cur," said the other, in a furious voice. "Let that be a lesson to you that Mohawk manners will not do for Hampshire. Dare to lay your hand upon this lady again and I'll thrash you within an inch of your life." "So it is you, Mr. Richard Davenant, is it? " said his lordship, scrambling to his feet and rubbing the back of his head as he did so. "I did not expect to find you in the Squire's house again, so soon. If I am not mistaken, he threatened, should you repeat your visit, to order his servants to flog you off the place. I will make it my busi- ness to acquaint him with the fact at once. Mistress Phyllis would enjoy the exhibition, I feel sure."

----- With this he reeled from the room, leaving the young couple together. For upwards of a minute neither of them spoke. The whole affair had been so sudden that in spite of Belsborough's threat they had not recovered their self-possession yet. It was Phyllis who spoke first. "Why have you come ? " she asked, clinging to her lover and looking up into his face with frightened eyes.

"Because I could not remain away from you," he answered, hoarsely. "Because, at any:risk, I knew I must see you again. I left my horse in the village, climbed the park wall, and was upon the terrace, listening to your music, when that man entered the room. I did not intend to come in, but when he laid his hands upon you I could bear it no longer, and acted as you know." "And now you must go," she answered. "If he should tell my father, as he threatens, there is no knowing what might happen. You do not know how angry papa is with you. He would kill you, I believe, if he found you here. You must go at once." Then in a choking voice she added: 'And you must promise never to attempt to see me again." "Phyllis," he cried, "is it possible that you can ask such a thing of me? What makes you say it There must be some reason for it I feel sure. Can you have forgotten our vows? Is it possible you have ceased to love me? " "I love you more fondly than ever," she replied, slowly and very sadly. "But my father has declared that on Christmas Eve, a week from to-day, whether I wish it or not, I shall marry the man you knocked down just now." "Marry Lord Belsborough ? " cried the young man, fiercely. "Such a thing is not to be thought of. lam poor enough, Heaven knows. The old home is mortgaged to the last penny, but at least I can offer you a name equal to your own and a man's true love. I would as soon see you married to Will Pringle, the highwayman, as to that man. You know his reputation and the villainous stories that have gone about concerning him." "I know everything," she answered, "but it can make no difference. My father has set his heart upon it, and when he has done that, it is, as you know, impossible to move him."

The young man drooped his head. He had pinned all his faith on one hope, and that had failed him. Three weeks before a venerable uncle in Northamptonshire had died, and by every mail the young man had expected to hear that he was his heir. No news, however, had so far come to hand, and he had therefore been compelled to face the irate Squire as a penniless suitor for his daughter's hand.

"If only Uncle Geoffrey's letter had come," he cried, bitterly, "we might never have been so unhappy. But I have received nothing, and your father scorns me as a poor man. What can I do? I am powerless."

"If you can do nothing, then I am lost indeed," the girl replied, but oh—" At this moment a sound reached their ears. "Hark They are coming," she cried. "Quick, you must go Do not hesitate a moment. if they find you here, we are both lost. For my sake, go." "But whatever happens, I must see you again.*

_ "When and where you please," she answered. He kissed her fondly, and then, bidding her good-bye, left the house by the way he had entered it. He had scarcely vanished before Lord Belsborough, followed by a couple of menservants, armed with hunting crops, entered the room. The former gazed about him with surprise at not finding his enemy present. "Gone away, fore 'gad," he cried. Then seeing the open window, he! added: "After him, lads, we'll run him to earth yet." They climbed out into the darkness, leaving the terrified girl alone.

Tim nights later Dick Davenant stood in the small copse that marked the southern boundary of the Squire's demesne. It wanted only a few minutes of eleven o'clock, but late as the hour was he:was not alone. His companion was no less a person than Judith Hartford, and strange to relate, she was seated upon one of the Squire's most cherished hunters. She had only made her appear- ance a few moments before, and was still endeavour- ing to prove to the young man standing before her the advisability of pursuing a certain course of action.

"If you are afraid," she continued, with a scorn which was perhaps all the more marked for the reason that she knew that it was undeserved, "I will do it myself. But, situated as you sare, I should think it was worth so much trouble." "I was not thinking of myself." _ _ . . _

"Of whom were you thinning, then ? " "I was thinking that it was scarcely seemly for you to be riding in this fashion about the country- side at this hour of the night." At this moment her horse became restless, and in her attempt to quiet him, her answer was inaudible. "You consent then ? " she said, when at last she had reduced her steed to something like order. "In that case, let us be going. You say you left your horse with Farmer Giles. You had better get him and join me at the cross roads." Without giving him time to reply she turned her own animal and rode out of the copse, heading towards a portion of the park where the wall was not so high as elsewhere. It was a beautiful star- light night, and the slight mantle of snow which covered the ground helped to make it lighter than it would otherwise have been. I have already said that she was a fearless and accomplished horse- woman. I am tempted to go even further, and to add that she was also reckless. Choosing her point she gave her horse a touch with the whip, and set him going at a fair pace at the wall. He respondtd to the call, gathered his legs well under him, and a few seconds later was in mid-air. When she had pulled him up on the other side, Phe patted his stalwart crest and then trotted him slowly down the road towards the village. At the cross-roads she was joined by Davenant, now mounted upon his own roadster. "How far is it to Bembrido Heath? " she

itiquiracl wkea they were making their way clown the road.

_ "Seven miles by the high road," he answered. "But why Bymbridge Heath?"

You will sae in proper time," she replied. "For the present, follow me. If things are as I suppose, we have no time to lose." For upwards of an hour they continued their journey. Towards midnight the moon rose and looked shyly down on the wintry landscape. Reach- ing the heath, for which they were steering, they halted and took up their position in a little wood, a hundred yards or so removed from the road. From this point they could see for upwards of a mile, themselves meanwhile remaining unseen. "I cannot think why you have brought me here at this time of night," said Davens.nt, a little pettishly. "How is it to affect my marriage with Phyllis? 7 "Wait and see," said Judith. "I have had my suspicions for a week past. John Noakes, the stableman, had more money lately than he has ever had in his life before, and that set me tLinking. I waited and watched and this is the result."

_ For upwards of an hour they remained under the leafless trees. The sold wind blew as though it would freeze the very marrow in their bones. Still, however, Judith woubi not give the sign for a move. She was evidently on the look-out for, something or somebody. Suddenly a black object made its appearance on the crest of a neighbouring hill. Both saw it at ths same moment.

"The London coach," said Judith, by way of an explanation. "Now, watch what happens." The coach had descended the hill and the driver was springing his horses at the foot, when suddenly a horseman made his appearance upon the road and almost simultaneously the vehicle came to a standstill. When, five minutes later, it proceeded on its way, the man who kad stopped it remained where he was until it had turned the corner and was out of sight. After which he also started off. Then Judith spoke. "I have always known you to be a good hunts- man, Mr. Davenant," she began. "Now, lam going to give you such a run as you have never had when following the hounds. Come along." So saying she touched her horse with the whip, and they started off across country in the direction they had come. The outward journey had taken upwards of an hour, the return lasted only half that time. Arriving at the village, Davenant left his horse as before, with Farmer Giles, and accompanied Judith into her benefactor's park. Once more they stood together in the small copse, and once more they waited. Their patience was well-nigh exhausted when the sound of a horse, approaching them at a steady trot, reached their ears. A few moments later a man came into view and cantered passed, through the fast falling snow- flakes, towards the stable.

"We have done enough," said Judith. "Now I think you maygo home and ponder over what you have seen." "But what am I to do ? "

"To-morrow morning at eleven o'clock you must be at the Hall," she answered. "You must bring with you as many of the passengers by to-night's coach as you can collect. When you tell them that they will probably recover their lost valuables, I do not think they will refuse." "And after that? " "I think you will marry Phyllis," she replied.

The group next morning in the Squire's justice- room was in many respects a curious one. It was made up of the Squire himself, who was in a by no means amiable temper, my Lord Belsborough, exquisitely dressed, and who took snuff with the grace for which he had so long been famous, of the Squire's attorney, who had been robbed on. the previous evening of a number of valuable deeds, the Squire's property, and several other members of the coach party. They were well advanced upon a recital of their grievances when the door opened, and Judith Hartford, accompanied by the Squire's daughter, entered the room. "How now, machrns ? " cried the Squire angrily. "What brings you here? We're busy, and 'tis no place for womenfolk."

"Perhaps it would interest you, Squire, to hear that there is someone present in your house at this moment, who can put you on the track of Will Pringle, the highwayman," said Judith, quietly.

The Squire, in a fury, bade her instantly produce that individual or suffer the pain of his severest displeasure, adding, with an oath, "that when he did catch the aforesaid Will, he should swing gallows high, next sessions, or he'd know the reason why." -

Upon this, Richard Davenant entered the room. The Squire, on seeing him, flew into an even greater rage than before, and ordered him to leave the house forthwith. When Judith, however, had whispered something in his ear, he changed his mind and bade him speak up. "What is it you have to tell me, sirrah ? " he inquired in a voice of thunder. "Let me have it at once, and then be off about your business."

"I have come to tell you, Squire," said Davenant, "that it is my belief that you have been grossly imposed upon. The man seated on the settle yonder, calling himself Lord Belsborough, is no more his lordship than I am. He's an impostor, a cheat."

The man accused started from his seat with an exclamation. The Squire rose also, livid with fury. He tried to speak, but in vain, _ _

"I repeat," said Davenant, simulating a calmness he did not feel, "that he is not the man he pre- tends to be, and if you will bear with me, I think I can prove what I say. I declare him to be no less a person than the man who has made the King's roads a terror these many months past, Will Pringle, the highwayman." _ - .

The Squire's wrath knew no bounds. He shook his fist at the young man and vowed that he had never heard a more monstrous story. Eventually, however, he was compelled by sheer weight of public opinion to hear witnesses. John Noakes, stableman in the Squire's employ, was immediately admitted to the room. He deposed that on several occasions during the past fortnight, he had saddled my lord's horse at a late hour, and he confessed that he had been induced by bribes to keep silence on the matter. On each occasion his lordship had been absent some hours, and once when he returned a black mask had fallen upon the cobbles of the stable yard.

"Out upon 'ee for lying rogue," cried the Squire. "To say that his lordship is Will Pringle, is to tell me I've been made a fool of. Produce better proofs, Dick Davenant, or I'll clap thee in the stocks for a lying _knave." _ _ _ .

Davenant accordingly turned and made a sign to a servant standing near the door. The man left the room presently to return carrying a small trunk which he placed on the table before the Squire. "That is the property of his lordship," said Davenant, pointing to the article in question. "It has come direct from his bed-chamber. Open it, Squire, and if it does not contain what he took from the coach last night, I will be content to be foresworn." "I forbid you to open it," cried his lordship, springing from his chair, his face as white as that of Mistress Phyllis beside him. "'Tie my:property, and I'll not have it touched."

The Squire looked from one to another as if undecided how to act. Already he had a half belief that the man he had entertained as his guest was not what he pretended to be. He had no desire, however, to do him an injustice.

"I am sorry to subject your lordship to the violence of these knaves," he said, "but if you will only permit the thing to be opened it will give them the lie once and for all. 'Tie only a matter of form, and I pledge your lordship my word I do not credit the stories they bring against thee."

Unfortunately, however, his _ lordship remained obdurate. His refusal to permit the bag to be opened could only have one effect, that of increas- ing the Squire's suspicions."

"Look at his face, Squire," cried Davenant. "He fears what you may find there. Open it and you will find that what I say is true." At length, in spite of his lordship's threats and remonstrances, the bag was opened and the con- tents stood revealed. It contained, to the amaze- ment of everyone, all the valuables:taken from the passengers of the coach on the previous night, several documents, the property of the Squire, and among other indiscriminate papers a letter from the lawyer of Davenant's deceased uncle, from which it appeared that the young man was the sole heir of the old gentleman's accumulated wealth.

It would take too long to relate how Will Pringle received his deserts, how the Squire relented, and how he was eventuaiLy persitaded to agree to the marriage of his daughter to the man he had once professed 80 heartily to despise. Let it suffice, however, that although many years have gone by, he has never had reason to repent his action in the matter. With his grandchildren. of whom he has

THE DAILY PAPER

Consider again that miracle of this century the daily papsr,—the columns of which furnish us with the doings and sayings of men all over the world absolutely up to each day's date! The expenditure of a halfpenny puts us in possession of the state of affairs in every "people and kindred and tribe and tongue." We live in continual touch with the whole race. A century ago,— a newspaper was a rarity, and its news,—outside the immediate area from whence it was issued, although it aas news,—was days or weeks or months behind time. And inoreover,a newspaper by its costliness was beyond the reach of the majority. To us to-day, the newspaper has become almost one of the necessities of life. But we should have discovered, a century ago,that life was both possible arid tolerable without it. It is an unspeakably wonderful product of the age. Our only regret must , be that so mighty an iustreineut of human information land influence should so often be found a prime agent in promoting the debasing and ruinous popular habits of betting and gambling. THE SAVING OF LABOUR. Again, what wonders this century has seen in the multiplication, and at the Same time, by curious paradox, the saving of labour ! When the power-loom came into vogue, the hand-loom workers rose in revolt, believing that their .6 hour " was come, and their liveli- hood destroyed. But the application of steam power to machinery has indefinitely multiplied the use of machinery, thereby extending manufacture, widening trade, increasing infinitely the occupations for the people, and magnifying their means of living. That labour is largely saved by the inventions of this closing age, every hcuse-wife knows as she uses her sewing machine; every clerk as he manipulates the typewriter ; every merchant as he uses the telephone as he sits at his desk : everybody, as he strikes a lucifer match, and remembers (if he ever does) that less than a century ago he would have been dependent for the kind. line of light or fire on flint and steel and tinder box. How can we hope to chronicle even the practical , mechanical, material achievements of this unparalleled Century? An age which for purposes of light has blown oth, the candle, and largely forsaken the lamp, to make Use of gas as means of illumination, and is now rapidly emoavrevrellaonuds age! development superseding gas by the adaptation of electricity, is a payliwotifiephhohtaovgerawitnessed u t neer t dig. v Dr Wallace says, "which is to the external form;" tas of nature what printing is to thought " : and which have conceived the " phonograph, which preserves and re- produces sounds as photography preserves and repro- duces forms ' :and the Rontgen Rays—which make many opaque things transparent, and open up a still newer world to the photographic art : and the use of anesthetics, making critical and severest surgery pain- less,— of antiseptics, which makes it more possible than ever in surgical operations to save life :—a century which has seen these practical wonders has no rival in the history of the race I Dr Wallace, in the book already referred to, furnishes a tabulated list of the great inventions and discoveries of the 19th Century, on tee one hand, and of all preceding :tees on the eter. He specifies 21 as belonging to this one century, and only 16 for all preceding ages, not only of the Christian era, but of the whole history of the world! Verily, this is, of a truth," This wonderful i century," even judged alone by ts more practical achievements : all of which tend to the improvement, the enlargement, the amelioration of human conditions: and therefore to the incalculable benefit and blessing of the world. Let us not fail to see, and to devoutly ack- nowledge, the hand of God in it all EMANCIPATION. Turning now to other of the conapicuous characteris- tics of this closing century, let us make note of this—it will probably live on the page of history as a supreme era of emancipation. "In IEO7 the slave trade was pro- hibited in the British Empire, and 26 years later slavery itself was abolished in all our Colonies. The names of Clarkson, Wilberforce,: Zackary, Macaulay, and i Thomas Powell Buxton will ever be immortal n that illustrious connexion. Russia eclipsed our achievement in 1861 by emancipating 23 million serfs. President Lincoln, in the beginning of 1863, issued his famous proclamation declaring the extinction of slavery. in the United States of America ; and millions were iiberated at the cost of that unparalleled civil war. Within the last 25 years Brazil has completed the story of slave em- ancipation. Lord Rosebery's words may be fitly quoted here. He speaks of the century as "an era of emanci- pation," considerable though not complete. Nations, as a rule, have been sorted iota boundaries more consonant with their aspirations and traditions than was formerly the case. The tyranny of sects, in Britain at any rate, has partially abated. The undue pressure of as government h diminished, Slavery has disappeared. All over the world there have been great strides towards freedom ; and though inadequate, they have been se
