MARRIAGE BY ROBBERY.

BY GUY BOOTHBY, Author of "The Lust of Hate," "Dr. Nikola,' "In Strange Company," "A Bid for Fortune,' "The Beautiful White Devil," &o.

"Fore George, miss, hold thy tongue. I'll hear no more on't. Marry his lordship, or I'll lock thee up until parson's ready with the book for thee. I 'cod! Things are coming to a pretty pass, when a chit of a girl beards her father to his faoe, and declares she'll have nought to do with the man he has chosen for her, but must needs go dilly- dallying after a coxcomb without a penny in the world."

Having thus expressed his opinion, the Squire tossed his formidable hunting crop in one corner, his hat into another, and then threw himself into his justice chair as if the situation had passed beyond the region of his comprehension. His wig was awry, his face was crimson with passion, and for a moment it appeared as if his rage would choke him. His terrified daughter stood before him, the picture of despair. Her hands were clasped, her head was bowed, and her bosom rose and fell in quick spasmodic gasps. On a high oak settle on the other side of the hearth sat a young man, dressed in the height of fashion. He possessed a not unhandsome counten- ance, which, however, was seamed With the in- delible markings of a dissipated career. It was not by sitting at home and smoking his pipe in the chimney-corner that my Lord Belsborough had achieved the reputation of being the wildest and most reckless member of the Mohawk and Hell Firs Clubs.

"Prithee hear me for a moment, dear Squire," said the latter in a smooth and silvery voice that to an acute ear had a trace of mockery in it. "I cannot help thinking that you are a trifle severe with pretty Mistress Phyllis. Nor do you pay a compliment to myself when you vow that she shall marry me against her inclination. At present she is stony-hearted, but perhaps when we are a little better acquainted she may not prove so adamant. Let us postpone consideration of the matter, there- fore, for a few days."

"Postpone or no postpone, my lord, the lass shall marry thee," replied the Squire, bringing his fist down with a heavy smack upon the table. "Since it is your lordship's pleasure to wait awhile, we'll do it, but I give thee fair warning, l'm a bad laggard! In a week it will be Christ- mas. On Christmas Eve she shall marry thee willy-nilly. Hearken to that, girl, and pay heed to what I say." "I cannot, papa, I cannot!" the girl replied, as if her heart were breaking. "Why will you force me to what will only break my heart ? "

.. "Because it is my wish," her father answered. "Parson shall tie thee to his lordship on Christmas Eve. I've pledged my word, and I'll hear no more of it."

Seeing that it was useless to protest, the poor girl left the room and made her way up the broad oaken staircase to her own apartment, where she abandoned herself to her grief. Everything seemed indeed hopeless. Only that morning her father had threatened the man she loved with the direst penalties if he dared to approach the house or speak to her again. Now, as if this were not enough, he had declared that the man she hated and feared above all others should become her husband within a week. She was powerless to resist, and at the moment it looked as if us help could 'possibly come to her. Tears were still coursing down her cheeks when the door opened, and a tall, dark- eyed girl, wearing a riding-habit, entered the room. She was not beautiful, she was not even handsome, but there was something in her face—a something difficult to define—that at once attracted the atten- tion of strangers and caused them to glance at her a second time, as if they were not quite sure that they had not made a mistake in their first estimate of her character. Save the Squire and his family, she had not a friend in the world. Her father had met his fate in a duel when she was but six years old, and her broken-hearted mother had followed him to the grave a year later. The Squire, who had been an old friend of her father's, had thereupon adopted her in order that she might be a play- fellow for his own little motherless girl, and from that moment she had been a member of his house- hold—controlling the kitchen, managing the servants, attending to the duties of the still-roan, and in a hundred different ways repaying the kindness that the Squire had at first somewhat grudgingly, it must be confessed, shewn to her. Eventually, however, she had overcome his pre. judices, and that herculean task once accomplished, he had become as fond of her as it was possible for him to be of anyone, save his own daughter. She was, among other things, an expert and fearless horsewoman, and this fact, perhaps, did as much for her with him as anything else. To be able to sit his most dangerous hunters, and not only to sit them but to keep them always in the immediate vicinity of the hounds, was to arrive at his favour by a short cut. More than once he had declared, with his customary vehemence, that there was only one woman in the world who knew anything about fox-hunting, and that one was his adopted daughter, Judith Hartford. "Why, sweet chick, what is the meaning of these tears ? " asked the new-comer, as she seated herself upon the bed beside the weeping girl. "When I left you this morning you were SS happy, as happy as could be." _ -

"Since then my whole life has changed," the Squire's daughter replied, in a voice that suggested that she thought it possible she would never know happiness again. "My father has threatened to horsewhip my poor Dick, Fhould he try to see me again, and he has also declared that, whether I wish it or not, I shall marry Lord Belsborough on Christmas eve. It is too cruel! Judith, it will kill me."

The elder girl placed her arms around her and endeavoured to comfort her. It was a hopeless task, howevet% From the day of hen birth the Squire's personality had impressed itself so thoroughly upon his daughter that she was fair to believe that, when he had once set his heart upon a thing, no human power could alter or avert its accomplishment. Mistress Ilartford, however, was of a somewhat firmer mould.

"Cheer up, my dearie," said she. "You must not let yourself be cast down so easily. For the moment everything seems black, but the clouds will lighten ere long. Remember it is when the night seems darkest that the dawn is nearest." "I can see no hope of any dawn," the unhappy girl replied. And then she added, for perhaps the thousandth time that day: "Oh, why does my father force upon me a marriage that can only break my heart? is there no way in which you 'can help me, Judith ? " Judith considered for a moment. She had her own ideas upon the subject, but she had no desire to arouse hope in the other's heart until ,she saw a fair chance of being able to gratify it. For the remainder of the day the Squire, after the scene in the justice-room, was in a by no means amiable mood, It is true, in his own fashion, he loved his daughter, and for this reason he could not help feeling, in his inmost heart, that ha had been cruel to her. Save for the introduction he had brought with him from London, Lord Bels- borough was a comparative stranger. His position in the world, however, was well known, and the fact, that in marrying her to him his daughter would be a peeress was sufficient to make the Squire desirous of securing him for a son-in-law at any cost to his daughter's feelings. Conscience,

A NEGLECTED GENIUS.

It is sad to reflect how often the world has given bul) a grudging recognition to its greatest geniuses, and sometimes none at all, and not until death comes to end the bitterness of neglect do men discover that a groat prophet has been dwelling among them unawares. Tell is the moral of the story contained in an article entitled " The Vicissitudes of Millet's Angelus," in the Januazy numbar of the " Temple Magazine." " ' The Angelus,' says the writer, the Rev. James Johnston, "which wile painted in 1859, was originally sold by the artist for about £7O to M. Feydeau, and after passing through several hands, the price ever advancing as the fare of the picture crews it was purchased by Mr J W Wilson for £1,520, " At the Wilson sale in 1881 it became the property cd M. Secritan, the French copper-king, who gave no lee! than £6,400 for it. But reverses compelled M. Seaetati to part with the whole of his magnificent collection of art treasures, and once more 'The Angelus' changed hands." Subsequently, after many other wanderings,io came into the possession of that well-known Parisian art lover,M. Chsucard, who paid something like £32,000 foI it. " What pathos there is in the fact," concludes the writer, " that at the present moment the triumphs et Millet's brush are realising enormous prices, while the artist himself struggled against the pressure of poverty. for the most part all his life long amidst his lonely Barbizon surroundings, painting with a sympathetic power, such as no other painter has shown, the life of rustic France."

THE new century can- The New Century. not be said to open very cheerfully. We are not, enjoying peace or doing brilliant deeds in war. The prophets are all in a gloomy mood. One asks if England will last the century. Another amp- lifies the idea that we shall soon lose our commer- cial and maritime supremacy,_and merely become a residential country for men of means who want pleasure and repose. A third assures us that Germany and America are beating us in engineer- ing and in industrial development. Afourth thinks welare given up to sport and money-making, and that a night of scepticism in religion and politics is settling down upon us. Moodiness about the future is, however, characteristically English. It was left in Queen's ELIZABETH'S time, when the saying as "When Heave is spun England's e was letters of the word indicating the initials a the Sovereigns. GEORGE ELIOT has re- ferred to men" whose best hope for the world is over that the world is near its end." But in spite of these fantastic vaticinations we may venture to assert that the new century has in it some elements of hopefulness and promise in every region of activity. In industry we see the effects of a better understand- ing, and strikes are fewer, whilst the wages and standard of •comfort of the working classes are higher. There are not many new industries, it is true, and in electrical appliances we are not so ad- vanced as other countries, owing to our conserva- tive ways and our resolution not to detach our- selves , too easily from the past. It is to this conservatism of what is tried and true that we owe our stability and our freedom from revolutionary movements, if there is often a price to be paid for even-paced and cautious advance. In science we are holding our own, and without any State endowment of research. Our scientific literature is as good as that of any other country in the world. In the arts we have made vast improve_ ments, and our taste and our cultivation promise to further advance in coming years. In literature we can claim a pre-eminence which increases every year. In history and fiction, in politics and phil- osophy, we are original, and our rivals are incon- spicuous. The new century opens more brilliantly in this respect than did the old one which is dead. If we have fewer literary giants, we have more men and women in the second rank than we had when the nineteenth century began, and our second rankers are superior to the first rankers of other countries. Our navy stands in its old proud position, if we can hardly say the same for our army, which is be- ing subjected to severe trials under absolutely new conditions. Our taxable resources increase, and. we can bear with ease what would have im- poverished our groat-grandfathers. It looks as if in the now century we might have to bear more burdens than will be agreeable, and shall have need of a national financier as much as we did when the old century had begun by adding to our taxes and our obligations. In national spirit there is no falling off, and the wails of the prophets are merely goads to further exertions. In politics the outlook is less hopeful. Political equality has somehow been checked, and hereditary and social claims are still preferred and recognised. Popular rule has many problems before it, and efficiency in public affairs must be attained, at whatever sacrifice of rank or persons. The moral tow of the people jis better in some respects, ifworse in others. Sports are healthier, if laxity prevails in business, and in political affairs efforts are being made to exact a higher standard of duty and purity in our public men. Education and licens- ing are still big problems, but eaoh is advancing to- wards a solution. In short, we meet the future I' without fear, but not without some qualms, It

IT was to be expected Another Conservative that the Conservatives Claim. would endeavour to claim all the credit for a Colonial policy which consists in uniting our scattered self-governments more closely to the Mother Country. It was equally certain they would represent the Liberals as hav- ing all along intended to allow the Colonies to cut themselves off from us and form independent Communities. But neither position is either accurate or historical. A plausible case can be made out, of course,for either contention by ignor- ing facts, recalling past criticisms and misrspre- seating every action taken by Liberal Governments. Two or three points, however, can be stated with some confidence in their accuracy. The first point is that there was a time when the two great parties held and practised the same policy—that of dealing with the Colonies as if detachment might some day arrive and would not be an unmitigated evil. The second point is that the idea of self-government was resented by the Con- servatives, who saw in it the sure source of ulti- mate separation, whereas it has been the salvation of true Colonial as well as of sound Imperial feel- ing. If Downing-street had gone on governing the Colonies in the selfish interests of this country there must have been rebellion. The third point is that a premature attempt to force upon our American Colonies some Imnerial responsibility did end in disaster, for which the Liberals do not hold themselves responsible. The fourth point to be insisted upon is that all the preparatory work of federation has been the result of what was, in the main, a Liberal propaganda. The outcome of the good seed sown is visible to-day, but it is mon- strous that the Conservatives should claim that it is their work, and that Liberal policy has been Unsympathetic, disintegrating, and adverse to federation. Politicians have short memories, and hence these claims impose upon the forgetful, and mislead the young and the ignorant. The building up of our Empire is not something done to-day or last year. It has been going on for fifty years or more, and during that time the influence of Liberal ideas and the policy of Liberal statesmen have been in the ascendant. The recent turn of domestic politics shows how narrow are the notions of the persons who claim to have consolidated the British Eiripire. It is a hundred years since Ireland was Imperialised, and it is dis- contented still. The idea of federating Groat

1

men who are to-day flinging up their caps about Australian Federation do not believe in the prin- ciple applied nearer home. They are strong for the time being, but not in close political sympathy with the politics of the Australian Government, which are LiberaL in main, and not Conservative.

gloriously beaten, because it and hostile to Omar Han union with the Mother Country in South African affairs. The new claim, in short provokes investigation. Instead of accepting it, We hope that politicians will carry their ipvestiga-

be what we make it, and the more searching our self-examination is the more we are likely to benefit by it. It is better to begin in doubt than to indulge in levity and boastfulness.

Irr those glowing tributes which have been paid to the progress of the nineteenth century, possibly sufficient mention has not been made of the ad- vance of journalism during that period. A kind of professional m odesty has needlessly influenced the many clever writers who have discussed the general subject of,improvement during the century. ma perfunctory way it has been told how the century has seen the "tax upon knowledge" removed, and the power of the Press multiplied tenfold by the ifivention of rapid machines; but little has bean said of the spirit in which journalism itself has been conducted during she hundred years which have come to a close. The omission, however, is in part made good by the action of the Times and other long- established papers in reprinting extracts from their issues at various periods during the century, and 3 allowing the reader to form his own conclusions ;11 as to the ideal of newspaper work which prevailed ' At different times. And nothing is more notice- , :tole than the gradual widening of view which has ken place from the beginning of the century, the recognition of multiform interests, the broader charities, the kinder view of opponents, the ad- mission of the fact that life is a greater thing than any one conception of it can be, the large-hearted tolerance which, with exceptions to be counted O n the fingers, may be claimed at the end of the century as a characteristic of the Press of the day. It is an interesting conjecture how far the twentieth century will have travelled before electricity has superseded steam as the motive power on the long-distance railways of the world. That it will soon drive the shorter lines is obvious enough, but the change of system on the great trunk railways will present engineering difficulties of nu mean kind, and, perhaps, financial problems of even greater magnitude. Yet in the opinion of Practical engineers, electricity is bound to prevail. From the point of view of e;ainomy alone it is obvious that it is far better that there should be a few large fires in great electrical stations scattered over the land than that over the various lines of the United Kingdom there should be scattered some 20,000 locomotives, each provided with an isolated and comparatively insignificant source of power, useless when the engine is standing at platform and in siding, extremely wasteful even when it is in motion, and doomed, moreover, to carry behind it a mass of fuel corresponding to two or three passenger carriages. Other advantages which have been dwelt on lately have not been lost sight of by those engaged in solving the problems of railway management, a task which, as a writer in" Blackwood's " rightly insists, becomes year by year one of greater perplexity. Indeed, " blaga's " well-informed contributor hints that, in view of labour difficulties and increase of cost on the one hand, and, on the other hand, competition with electric undertakings, which naturally hesitate at alliance with existing companies burdened with over-capitalisation, the nationalisation of railways, notwithstanding all the objections which can be raised to it, may some day be almost welcomed by the proprietors of the older lines.

" Sunrise" is dead, and we regret the demise of so philanthropic and unselfish a little, journal. Twelve years have elapsed since its birth, and twelve eventful years in the history of the town they have been. We note from the " sunset " number that "our pleas in almost every instance have prevailed," and then follows a list of public improvements which " Sunrise " very modestly takes credit either for suggesting or urging to fruition. Inferentially the town would still be fifty behind the times were it not for " which appears to have taken under its wing. "The Dawn," a monthly magazine so that there will still be an outlet for the superfluous cash and ideas which the proprietors appear to be blessed with. Having indulged in a little sarcasm, we cordially pay tribute to the high ideal which the editor of and contributors to the pages of " Sunrise" have always kept before them, and we are equally ready to admit that the labours there- in expended for the advancement of the town and the furtherance of social progress have been by no means in vain. " Sunrise " has often deserved a better appreciation, which we hope its successor will just as fully merit and more fully obtain.

Another ship canal is being talked about—and not to Birmingham either. Yankee capitalists are struck with the advantage it would be to connect Southampton with London, and thus land passen- gers and freight fresh from the States right in the heart of the metropolis. The route has already been surveyed with the result, it is declared by those interested, that "no great engineering diffi- culties present themselves," and they believe the canal could be constructed for a "comparatively small outlay." We wonder what that is. An outlay small as compared with that on the war, or one not nearly approaching the national debt, perhaps? Compared even with the ex- pense of the Manchester Canal, that of one be- tween Southampton and London to accommodate vessels of vastly greater size than those which can make use of the existing inland waterway, must be enormous. And, of course, engineering diffi- culties do not "present themselves," beforehand. The scheme is attractive, no doubt, but it will not fascinate the public like the Manchester project did years ago. Investors have had an object lesson since then. A. canal to Birmingham was seriously proposed not long ago, but the idea fell through, and we do not see any bettet fate for the South- ampton-London venture unless the Yankees, who perceive so clearly its advantages, also find the meney.

MATTERS OF_ MOMENT. A Provincial Letter,

' Several important Actor Parliament came into operation with the New Year. First and foremost I may be mentioned the Companies Act, due largely to the labours of the late Lord Chief Justice Russell. That Act is expected to put a check upon the dishonesty which grew up under the old law. It deals with bogus qualifications of Idirectors and the gift of paid-up shares, prescribes that the prospectus shall not be a tissue of false- hoods, and that it shall state the minimum amount on which the board will go to allotment, while three-fourths of the total amount due on applica- tion must be paid up, bona-fide, before the com- pany starts business. The Act should reassure the investing public. Another uew measure is the Agricultural Hollings Act, whica simplifies the procedure for the assessment of compensation. The agricultuiist should find the law regarding his holding greatly improved, and many of the griev- ances caused by the exaction of penal rents remedied. The Burial Act, which came into force on rue iday, will produce many changes, as the re- sult of an agitation which had been continued ever since the passing of the Act of 1880 Some of these changes,however,until the several Burial Acts have been consolidated, many cause greater perplexity than before, The new Act only relates to ceme- teries, and therefore, does not remove any of the complaints relating to churchyards left unredressed by the Act of 1880. It is,moreover,not retrospective. It is chiefly around the question of consecration that the battle has been fought—and won by Non- conformists.

--- The dawn of the New Century puts the finishing stroke to the measure of last year which was received by all parties with the greatest amount of gratification, in being marked at the Antipodes on Tuesday by the birth of the Australian Common- wealth. Though prepared by the Colonies, the measure was introduced by the Gavernment, Only one controversial point was raised at the time, and that was amicably settled. The names of the states- men composing the first Ministry were on the last day of the century submitted to the Governor- General by Mr Barton,and it is satisfactory to know that Sir William Lyne accepted a portfolio. The Ministry is a strong and representative one, and its first year's work will be watched with interest by practical men in the English provinces and fedora- tionists everywhere, as well as by the Colonists of Augtralia. The historic and inaugural procession at Sydney on New Year's Day had been designed on an elaborate scale, but the work of preparation had taken so much time that the first hours of the Twentieth Century were trenched upon in rounding off the decorative material.

And when did the twentieth century begin? This • was the question thrust upon newspaper readers, by the correspondent of a London contem- porary, at the vary moment when some of them I must have been congratulating themselves on having escaped a revival of the con- troversy of the previous year, and has since been taken up by certain provincial writers. Suffice it to say that the point now raised is in itself novel and that this is the only plea for reverting to it here. The writer in the " Morning Post " begins with the statement that the two great divisions of historical times are B.C. and A.D., and then goes on to say that history and theology are for all practical purposes agreed so far as to celebrate the birth of Christ on the 25th of December, and that this is the date accepted by all Christendom in consequence. The great dividing line, being then, at the birth of Christ, and the , birth of Christ being celebrated unquestioned on thel 25th December, that it necessarily follows the 25th December and not the Ist of January, was the first day of the new century, and that it was the first day of the first century A.D. He then proceeds to hammer home his contention, and is evidently making converts of some who are easily persuaded. It seem late in the day for a controversy to be waged around the question of the eligibility of women to sit on School Boards; but it is a ques- tion which has been much discussed ot late, and the position of those who take what may be assumed to be the popular view does not appear particularly strong, A writer in the " Law Journal" appears to be strongly of opinion that ladies can- not legally become members. Another suggests that probably some day, when a chance occurs, there will be a clause inserted in some future Act of Parliament similar to the clause in the Local Government Act, passed March sth, 1894, as to guardians, namely :--" No per- son shall be disqualified by sex or marriage for being elected or being a guardian." Men and women would then, he adds, be clearly and beyond all dis- pute, on the same legal footing as to School Boards. It may have been noticed that in Whitaker's Almanack for 1901 just published,at page 669,under the head "School Board Elections," there are the words, "No qualifications necessary, but aliens, &c., are disqualified. So, too, are women, but they are sometimes elected without objection."

For many hours of the past week the whole country may be said to have been without useful' telegraphic communication, owing to the gales. In case of emergency through invasion, actual or threatened, or in case of any other condition re- quiriog promptitude of action or decision, when every minute might be worth hours, as the saying goes, our resources have been in a condition of complete paralysis. The furtherance of commercial convenience might be made to coincide in a re- markable manner with precautionary provision for the national safety, if only the Chambers of Corn- merce.scattered over the k ingdom would unitedly endeavour to secure such object.. it assuredly behoves these organizations, in conjunction with local authorities to take the matter up, by urging as strongly as possible that some, at least, of the principal lines of communication should be laid underground, for the sake of greater security from accidents by storm. So practical a reform should not be difficult of attainment, and need not be too costly, considering the enormous permanent bene- fits that would be conferred on the community. At any rate London, the heart of England and the Empire, ought to be placed in perpetually safe communication with our principal seaports and arsenals and military depots, and with the shore ends of the submarine cables.

--- Apart from what relates to South Africa, the pro- vincial outlook may still be considered favourable, from„the business point of view. Some months since there were reasons to fear that the prosperity enjoyed for several years was almost at an end With the termination of the war, it was predicted by the pessimists that depression of trade would Fome, and the prospects in the coal and shipping industries were regarded as particularly dark, But,

so far from the " boom" in the shipping trade having spent itself, the shipbuilders on the Clyde and in the north-east ports have, within the past two months, been inundated with orders; while the Welsh coal-owners have entered into some large contracts at good prices. '.the existing slight depres- sion in the iron trade, there is reason to believe, is but temporary The building and other outdoor trades, owing to the continual mildness of the weather, are showing increased activity and becom- ing brisk.

An enjoyable concert followed. SHOCKING ACCIDENT AT OLD QUAY. . . _ . .

A shocking accident occurred at -the Manure Works of the United Alkali Company at Old Quay, Runcorn, on Wednesday afternoon about four o'clock. An electric motor works the crane which ,is used for loading and discharging ships in the Ship Canal and so rapidly does the machine work that it is necessary to constantly chalk the strap. On Wednesday afternoon Alfred Smith, a young man of 21 years,whose home is in Percival-lane at- tempted to perform this duty while the machinery was in motion. His arm became entangled and it was quickly sawn off below the elbow by the rapidly revolving strap. Every possible attention , was shown to him and he was conveyed by the first available train to Liverpool Infirmary. New Postmaster for Runcorn.—A Warrington Man Appointed. Mr W Bennett, the assistant superintendent at the Warrington Post Office has been appointed post- master of Runcorn. For upwards of 25 years Mr Bennett has been connected with the Warrington Office, His services commenced in 1875 and oy his assiduity and integrity he gradually rose step by step until in 1892 he was appointed assistant super- intendent. He has now been promoted to the position of post :,aster at Runcorn and he will enter upon his duties there on the 14th instant. Mr. Bennett replaces Mr Orrell, who has been removed to Patricroft. RUNCORN NOTES.

[By a Correspondent].

That there is arsenical beer in the Runcorn Rural District is abundantly proved by a letter from Mr R Potts, clerk to the County Council, to the Run- corn Rural District Council. For out of seven samples taken and analysed one of beer showed I about I-500th part of arsenical acid per gallon. True this is a small amount; but it must not be for- gotten that it establishes the fact that "poisoned beer is on tap, or rather has been on tap; and, if there is a trace of arsenic in the brew,it establishes the possibility of a bigger dose creeping in. In a letter which is in a degree a criticism of Sir C Quilter's Pure Beer Bill (written to the District Council of Runcorn) Sir J T Brunner deals with a very important aspect of the question. The letter is published in another column.

Mentioning Sir J T Brunner's name reminds me that he is due at the Liberal Club,Runcorn,on Tues- day next the Bth inst.,when he will address all who care to hear upon Liberal politics.

-- Another interesting subject before the Board of Guardians was the better treatment and classific- ation of aged deserving poor. Who is there that will not hail the advent of better times for the poor with feelings of pleasure. A point to pride our- selves upon, however, is the fact that the Runcorn Guardians have acted in the spirit of the Local Government Board for some time past. The grant- ing of out-relief sufficient to make independent of charity will, it is hoped, enable Runcorn to avert what was done in another parish. Here an aged couple were taken "to the Fiouee " and the husband and wife separated by the distinction of" Men and VVomen's Wards," when both were well on the way to 70 years of age.thaugh their poverty was brought about by no fault of their own.

Runcorn almost secured a maiden Sessions, on Monday last, but fate and Detective Sergeant Gregory intervened. Had it come about, however, I think it would have been due tip the fact that stuumonses were not made returable, rather than as an indication of the " state of crime " in the district. Considering the time of the year, though. one cannot help thinking that intemperance is on the decline in the town, for the festive season just concluded has not been productive of" drunks." " Arsenical Beer" may be the cause, hut personally I incline to the belief that a higher tone pervades the inhabitants, who as a class are sober,industrious and religious.

Mr. Thos Smith, the captain of the " Dagmar " who accomplished the gallant reicue of three War- rington men during the recent gale, has a record as a life saver of which any man might well be proud. Still, such is the irony of fate, his past exploits have been their own reward, and he still awaits due official recognition. Tie has also the reputation of being a capable, trustworthy seaman and well able to pilot the Mersey on the darkest of nights.

FOOTBALL. Runcorn's performances during the holidays have been good,and since last I wrote they have defeated Leigh and Birkenhead Wanderers. This too,in face of the fact that ths redoubtables, Faulkner and Butterworth have been unable to don the jersey for almost the whole of the season. Next week they face the Rochdale Hornets, at Rochdale, an event they should carry off if any thing like present form 'is shown. Bertie Joaes,the new venture, is proving a useful fellow at three-quarter, and has quite come up to expectations. Indeed, some think he has the making of a "Crompton," and as it must be con- ceded that the Runcorn critic know something about football, a few years might see the prediction verified.

LOCAL HARRIERS.

RUNCORN v. WINNINGTON.

The!visitors to Runcorn on Saturday were the Win- nington Harriers. These lusty athletes came without the services of two of their usual counters, while Run- corn were without such men as Cadman, Ray, Lewis, Stubbs, Povey, and Temperley, who had not as yet got over the previous Saturday's engagement, which was a nine miles road race. Six of the Ruocorn lads turned up to help to sustain the honour of the Runcorn club against fifteen of the Winningtoo pack. It was decided to count the first five in each tearn. The hares, Hand- ley and Kilgary, and one of the visitors, laid the trail over some heavy country in the direction of Rock Savage, Sutton Weaver, flail Wood, and home, a distance of seven miles. Thomas, of the homesters, tuok up the running from the start, and made things hum for about four miles, when Sims, of the visitors, came to the front and led for a while, about a mile from J Goodall, of the homestors, who eventually passed Sims, and had no diffi- culty in getting first home. He was followed by Sims, ' of the visitors, and close to the latter come Memos, of the Runcorn pack. foe race became rather keen, as the counters of each team arrived very close up, and ended in a victory for Winnington by the narrow margin of the three points. fhe teams finished in the following 'order : —Runcorn : Goodall, Ist • Tnomss, 3rd ; Leyland, sth ; Matthews, 9th ;Filkine, o:h—total, 30 points. Win- nington : Sims, 2ad ; Manderville, 4th ; Eiwards, etti Hodgkinson, 7th ; Booth, B,h—total, 27 points. On Saturday next the fixture is an inter-club run with the Warrington Harriers at Warrington.

The Chairman before commencing the routine business of the Council said he wished them all a happy new year, and asked them to join him in wishing the town, on the threshold of a new century, health, wealth and progress. HEALTH OF THE TOWN. The Medical Offic:ir reported the number of deaths registered during tke past month amounted to 27. To were the subjects of inquests. The average was 17 and exclusive of inquest cases 15 per 1,000 of the estimated population, 46 births were reeistered. For the corres- ponding period of last year 26 deaths, and 53 births were registered. The town was free from infectious diseases.

HIGHWAYS AND BUILDINGS. Mr 8 Taylor proposed the adoption of the Highways and Buildings Committee recommendations, by which the Council undertook to plant trees on the North side of Moughland-lane at the expense of the occupiers of such road.

MARKETS AND CEMETERY COMMITTEE. ' This committee recommended that the following report prepared by the secretary as to the execution of the Canal 'hats Acts be approved and adopted : " Twelve boats have been registered during the year. Four were new boats and eight re-registered. The total number of boats registered at Runcorn is 1,023. Twenty-four contraventions of the Acts and Regulations have been reported by the Canal Boats Inspector. The owners were communicated with in each instance, except with regard to the cases hereafter mentioned, and the Inspector reported from time to time to the Committee that the matters complained of had been remedied. Legal prc.ceedings 'were taken against the mast 'r of the boat "Adriatic" for allowing such boat to be used as a sleeping place by himself, wife, and son, about 25 years of age. Proceedings were also taken against the master of the boat "Wyoming" for allowing his boat to be overcrowded, the boat having three chil- dren too many on board. Penalties were inflicted in each case by the Justices. Toe registration of throe boats has been cancelled. 301 boats have been insoected luring the year, on board of which were found 447 men 194 women, and 157 children.' The Council again noted with regret that there was an incrsase in the number of women and children found on board the boats inspected. No case of infectious disease was notified as having occurred on a canal boat.

TEE BURIAL ACT.

The Cemetery Committee also reported that the Burial Act, 1000, came into force on January let, 1001. The two main features of the Act an :—The re-distribution between the Secretary of State and the Local Govern- ment Board of the powers and duties of a Central Authority under the laws affecting burial,and the altera- tion of the law regulating consecration, chapels,and fees payable to ministers, clerks, and sextons. The Committee also recommended that the table of ministers fees no* in force be submitted to the Secre- tary of State for Home Affairs for his approval. EXIT 1900-WELOWE 1901.

Runcorn.

With characteristic pantomime effects, " the man in the Runcorn Streets " heralded the change of the year ; bells were clangieg,hooters hooting and whistles screech- ing, but with an "accordin" as he lovingly calls it,there was a class of "man in the street" in Runcorn, who easily eclipsed pandemonium, and caused the " three furies ' pangs of jealousy, without himself " turning a hair." New Year's Eve found him on the prowl, and those who did not hear him, had either spent too good an evening, or were comfortably laid to rest some time ago in the pretty Greenway-road Cemetery. Why such spirits ce.n find no other vent is matter for wonderment? Rowdyism, on any other hand, however, was conspicuous by its absence, and though large crowds paraded the streets wishing one another boisterous "good wishes," the whole town was pervaded with a high tone and the "stagger- ing roysterer " was less in evidence than ever. Probably this was due to the efforts put forward by the various places of worship where midnight services were held ; and, to this, also, can be att:ibuted the fact that on the eve of a New Century almost a representative of every Runcorn family was gathered within the precincts of a place of worship seek- ing th 3 guidance of God in the future, which shall lead to that " bourne" of which Shakespere so feeling speaks when he says "from which no traveller returneth." The details of these services will be found below. Thl Bind of the 2nd Earl Cheshire (Runcorn Detachment) played through the streets, their usual " waits," and on the whole gave excellent programmes. Carol singing was general, the youthful element of the town being espec- ially promicenc, The most enjoyable street item, how- ever, was the performance of an impromptu "Young men's choir," composed largely of the Runcorn male voice party, who alter the several places Of worship had " let-out," went round the town rendering really capital items. Those who heard them enjoyed the singing, and desire to thank the singers.

Service at St. Paul's Church, Runcorn.

The last night of the 19-h Century was most fittingly observed by the worshippers of St. Paul's,Church Run-- corn, and was conducted on the lines laid down for the Wesleyan Metialcist Twentieth Century celebration, and similar to that held in abut 8,030 Wesleyan places of worship throughout the Kingdom. Cosi-mishear:lg at 10 o'clock with "0 for a thousand tongues to sing "to the good old tune" Lineham," the service was kept up in- spiringly until 12.10, and its conclusion left an impres- sion, if not lasting, long ta be remembered by all who were present. N3ver, perhaps, have the walls of St. Pantie given shelter to so large an audience congregated for a similar function, and not many are the occasions whereon so large a bole, of psople were so feelingly moved. The hymns which were sung with true congre- gational zest included:—"Ail hail the power of Jesus name," "I'll praise my maker while I've breath," "Salvation 0 the j3yful sound," "Let the earth and Heaven agree," "Rock of ages cleft fcr me," and "Come let us anew our journey pursue," The selected passages ' of Scripture were Psalm ciii., and the " Second lesson." Ton Rev II Merrisoa founded hie address on "My times are in Thy Hand," and the R3V, a 13 Foster chose " Sl teach us to number our days that we may apply or hearts to wisdom," and the Rev A Wells selected " Ye shall henceforth turn this way no more," as sub- ljecte for profoundly impressive addresses. Mr R H Posnett and Mr T Simister led the prayers When the congregation rose to sing the New Year hymn. the Rev A. Wells gave them the "New Year's Greeting." Referr- ing to the 20th Century Lind, he said that originally the Methodists of the Runcorn Circuit had promised to raise 1330 guineas. Of than ameunt more than £l3OO had been raised. (Hear, hear.) At the recent quarterly meeting of the circuit,it was resolved to raise Runcorn's pledge to 1600 guineas. (Hear, he.ar.) An 1 of that amount he bad great pleasure in stating that 127 guineas were-premised towards the addit'onal amount asked for. He concluded with an appeal for the balance. The St. Paul's choir was augmented by members from Halton- road, and Camden. Mr. Reggie Johnson presided at the organ. Ties whole eerv‘ce was thoroughly comforting, hearty, brillantly enjoyable and inspiring,

The Parish Church. The service here was conducted by the Vicar, the Rev Canon A M Wood, M.A., and was very largely attended. The hymns sung were ." A few more years shall roll and" Just as I am without on plea."A masterful address was also given by the Vicar on the " Past, Present, and Future." The service was most impressive.

Halton Wesleyan Trinity Church. An exceptionally large congregation attended the mid- night service at this place of worship, wtnch lasted from quarter to eleven until live past twelve. Helpful ad- dresses were delivered by Messrs. James Looker and W Moores. Several appropriate hymns were also sung.

New Year's Treats at Runcorn. The usual New Year School Treats were given to the scholars of Brunswick, Camden and Nelson-street Wes- leyan Schools, on Tuesday and Wednesday evenings.

Earlestown and Newton.

The last day of the century passed over very quietly indeed in this neighbourhood. All the works, with the exception of the printing and paper establish- ments at Newton were close] and there was conse- quently a good number of people present in the streets. Many people left the town for the important centres such as Manchsster, and Liverpool, but at night the streets wore a very busy aspect, caused chiefly by wor- shippers attending the watch night services at the var- ious churches and chapels, all of which were crowded. References were made in all the places to the dying century and the possibilities of the New Century. On the stroke of midnight rockets were Sent up, and- buzzers blown heralding the New Year, and cornea tula tions were numerous on all sides.

New Year's Parties at Earlestown. Wesleyan Sunday School.

The annual tea, entertainmen,t and distribution of prizos in Connection with the above school took place in the schoolroom on Tuesday. About 300 were present to tea the tables being presided over by teachers and lady members of the congregation. In connection with the young men's Bible c'ass there were 123 on the books with an average attendance of 63. The Juvenile Missionary Association had collected £4O which was devoted to the Home and Foreign Missions. Towards the Million Guinea Fund the scholars had collected £39 18a 73, the young men's bible class £49 184 teachers £39 18s, total £125 14s 7d. Last year the scholars collected £6 7s for Dr Stevenson's home for destitute children. The net income of the school was £Bl OS 111, and expenditure £73 19s leaving a balance of £7 12s 0. Bewsey Road Wesleyan Church.

An impres-ive feature of the watcenieht service at this Church, was the interpretation, by the choir,of a special choral servize camporipd by Dudley Buck. 'fnere was a good congregation, tue Rev. C Hllockm coedusting the proceedings. Tuis service opened with the hymn " Sal- vation, 0 the jsyful sounj, ' which was foilowed by a suitable prayer. The first portion of the choral service was then gone through, commencing with the beautiful chorus " Lard Taou hast been our refuge." The hymn " Rock of Ages " was heartily sung, after which the Rev. C. El. Rucked delivered an ao-.rose from the words " And he that sat upon the throne: I will make all things new." During tlie course of his remarks he said that the churches were inaugurating great movements in a great ceatury. Their own church was raising one million guineas, and the Free Church Federation of Ragland and Wales was inaugurating • great mission. What were they going to do? Were they looking tor ward, and would their life be renewed with new interests in the period betere them ? God would take their chant life and make it new. Th../ should enter the new century with a new heart. The choir then rendered the second portion of the cantata and the last few minutes of the old centary and the first few m.niites of the new were spent it, silent prayer. Tne service was brought to a nnclusita with the singing of the chorus " Glory be to Gad an high." Mr B Wadsworth ably presided at the organ alio he is to be complimented upon the success which attended the musical portion of the service. A coilection was taken on behalf of Dr Stephensou'e homes for waifs and strays. Wycliffe Church.

The service at Wycliffe Church was conducted by the Rev John Yonge. The hymns sung were : " Praise my soul, the King of Heaven," "L He comes with clouds descending," "On, safe to the reek which is higher than I," " Onward Christian Soldiers." Shortly betore mi I- night tire pastor delivered a brief address. Be remarked that they were indebted to the Methodists for the more religious observance of this particular period. If his recollection did not deceive him, in his youeger days it was mainly a Wesleyan custom to have a is archnignt service. John Wesley mast have taught his followere to rescue so suggestive a season from mere frivolities, and to turn it to some bettor &exeunt. and for a long time,he thought, the Wesleyan had a kind of monopoly of this service. During the las^ 30 or 40 years, however, their example bad teen followed by other churches, until at length even the Anglican chumhes and cathedrals bad been opened for such services, and were open that night. As a matter of fact, the places of worship in the country where such a service was not being held were very few and far between. The mei. sion partook of a tinkles and distinctive character. They were not only watching the close of one year and the beginning of another, but they were watching the last moments of the century and the in- coming of the new century. That to them was abso- lutely unprecedented and certainly could never for theta Ibe repeated. There was a great deal of impressiveness lin standing, as they did, on the threshold of centuries, upon the farther:nort boundary of one and toe earliest door of the other. They would cease talking of the 19th century as a present period in a few minutes* It would be as completely past as all its predecessers. Toe mighty tome of its marvellous history would be closed and from. pleted.and the angel of the infinite rewords would beein with tireless nen indinng the perpetual story of God's ways with this world in the 20i:h century. A n-w volume would be opened in Heaven. Who could prospect its cern. tents? If they were in Christ Jesus,as he,prayeo God they might be, it would be a glorious century for them The specially historic character of the occasion being the ending of the century might serve to send their reflec- tions back over the whole of their past life, retie ring not so exclusively a closing year, as was customary now but all the years. Mingled emotions must ever tr stirred by such e retrospect—gratitude, regret and penitence. In conclusion he asked the memtnn of the congregation to prepare for the new year end the remaining days of the years of life by prevnt self- surrender to the Saviour and King. The last few moments of the old year were sneut in silent prayer, and when the hour had struck Mr Yonge wished all a happy and prosperous new year and century. A hymn was sung, and the service concluded with the Benediction

St. Peter's Church.

The service at SC Peter's Church was conducted by the Rev W Bracecamp in the pre;ence of a very large gathering of parishioners. Appropriate hymns were heartily sung,and the reverend gentleman, in the course of s short address from the words " For God as loved the world that He gave His only begotten Son,that who. soever believetb in Him, should not perish, but have everlasting life," said that if there was any time during the year when one ought to be ?erious,it was during the last few moments of an expiring year and ths first moments of a new year. When they looked bark upon the pest 12 months,and thought of their sins of omission and commission, if they were men and women at all they must realiz3 that they were guilty towards God for the sins they had committed in thought, word and deed during the past 12 months and during the years in which they had been privileged to live. It was sad to reflect that in a parish like that, contsining 10.000 people, they could barely scrape up 400 on a Sunday night. He hoped their consciences would smite them, and that they would never rest until they saw better attendances at church during the coming year. While the congregation knelt in prayer, the preacher asked for a blessing upon the town, the municipal authorities, the absent vicar, the congregation, organists members of the choir, and himself, and when the new century dawned he wished all present "a very blessed aud prosperous new and happy year in the true sense of the word "—happy because they had realized where to get true happiness from. Might God bless them in their ' going-out and coming-in.

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES

UNSOUND HAMS AT WARRINGTON—SMARM FINES INFLICTED. At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, before Mr. F. Monks (presiding) and other magistrates,Charlesl Lupton, provision derler, 31, Winwick-street. was sum- moned for exposing for sale 17i hams, weighing 1801 pounds, which were unfit for human food. Mr. Browne appeared on behalf of the defendant. —lnspector Norman stated that on the 'lsth lest, between 11 and 12 o'clock in the forenoon, he visited the defendant's shop, having perceived two or three pieces of ham, which he., suspected were net in a sound condition. He lookedl at one of the pieces and also at a number of other hams which were hanging up in the shop. On Cutting soma of them open he found that they stank and were decomposed, which made them quite I unlit for human food. The deputy medical officer of health examined them and pronounced them to be unfit for food, and Mr Hayward, a magistrate, gave an order for the hams to be deatroved.—For the defence Mr Browne pointed out that 11-r Lupton bought the hams from a Liverpool gentleman, He obtained a stock of the highest class Armour's hams, three weeks a.m. Re sold some of them and he had no complaints and the re- mainder were hung in the shop. When Inspector Nor- man visited the premises the hams were not concealed, and there was no sign of unsoundness on the outside, Mr Lupton had been in busineas for twenty two years as a butcher and had had no complaints whatever previously. It was inspessble to tell whether the hams were in a bad ron-lition or not until they were cut iato.Ele submitted that therewas no guilty intention on the part of the detendant.The Act of Portia: merit provided ,that the person should have the know- ledge, or neglect to take necessary nrecautions. D— fendant never sold hams until he had cut the-n open or tested them with a skewer.—The Bench were satis- fied that the defendant was guilty and fined him £5 and costs. In coming to this decision they considered it an important fact that a portion of the meat seized was only half a ham. NORTFIhRN UNION. LANCASWRE SENIOR COMPETITION.

Warrington Finish_ the Century with

a Win.

Warrington recorded anoth3r v;ctory over Rochdale Hornets on Saturday, at the Athletic Grounds, Roch- dale, and thus completed the Century's engagements satisfactorily. The game was a very close one, and 'victory only accrued to Warrington by the small margin of one point. Both sides were without several regular players, Warrington having to find substitutea for Fish, Isherwood, Mereweather, Taylor, Morrisoo, and Duck- worth. The weather was fine, and a crowd of about 5,000 people witnessed the maacti. Heavy rain during the week had made the turf very se% and good football was almost an impossibility. A poor game was the natural result. Mr. F. Kennedy, of Broughton, was the referee, and had charge of the following teanis : Warrington : Hallam, full-back ; Ratcliff3, Hocken- hall, Dickenson aud Harris, threa-quarter4; Burgess and Scott, half-backs; Boardman, Schoitzi, Eden, Fairhurst, Swift, Jolley, Pemberton and Pennington. forwards. Rochdale Hornets : Rigg, full-back; Richardson, Roe- buck, Field and Wightman, three-quarters ; Mason and Grindred, ha f-backs •, Trembath. Langhorn, Coop3r, Greenhalgh, Howarth, Berrill, Kershaw, Jardine, for- wards. Tne Hornets started the gains and midfield play followed.Warrington heeled out well,and Burgess dodged through, passing to llocksnhall who sent slightly for- ward to Ratcliffe when the latter had a clear run in. Two minutes later, more really excellent cimbinatioa by the Warrington halves and centres gave Harris a , chance at tha opposite corner and he was all but over when Field grabbed birn by the jersey. A fine forward rush by the Hornets carried play into the Warrington half, and Burgess being penalised Field kicked a good goal. This might easily have been the winning point, but the Warrington men made another effort while they were still fresh. Burgess and llockenhall opened the game out and Ratcliffe took the ball on the run. When challenged by Rigg he deftly punted over the full back's bead, followed up and scored a really clever try. The • same player tried to augment but the heavy ball fell Ishort of the goal. Up to half-time play was of a uninter- esting character and neither side could claim any

advantage. The second half opened badly for Warrington, Scott injuring his leg early on and having to be carried off the field. Eden came out of the pack and Warrington went In for strictly defensive tactics. The Hornets kept pegging away on the Warrington quarter line, but their efforts wore so poor that the visitors' defence was seldom seriously taxed. Field had several shots at goal which failed, while his play at centre to Wightman was awful. The latter nearly pulled the match out of the fire by speedy following up, for he beat llallarn in the race, but the ball rolled dead. Towards the close War- *ington worked their way to the home half and Ratcliffe made a smart effort, losing the ball when be had the line at his mercy. There was no further scoring and Warrington retired winners by a try (three points) to a goals (two points.)

Runcorn v. Leigh.

Over 3,000 people assembled at Irwell Lane, on Saturday, when the above teams met. The perform- ances of previous years merited the anticipation of a capital game, and though Runcorn can claim to be superior Leigh have often upset their finest calculations by securing a winning point. Warder receivingi from Richardson set Di Jones on the move, and much ground was gained. Knight saved with promptitude. Racing away, the home forwards made several dashes for the Leigh line. Leigh's defence was too good to be trifled with, however. Jolley had an abortive shie at goal. Boardman was replied to by Houghton, and Ganby mulling the ball got neatly col- lared. The move brought out a perfect opening for D. Jones, but at the critical moment his usual resourceful- ness failed him. Leigh returned play, and then the game lagged through too much kicking and marking being resorted to. Later, Houghton put his side on the aggressive. Further advantage came through Richard- son and Jones, and shortly after a penalty gave Jolley a second shot at the uprights which he again missed. 'A" TEAMS COMPETITION. --- Warrington V. Rochdale Hornets.

-- - After Warrington's disappointing exhibitions of late,it was not anticipated even by their most ardent sup. porters that the encounter with the Rochdale second string would result in victory. As it was, Warrington suffered defeat to the extent of thirtenn points to three. About 1,500 spectators assembled on the Wilderspool en- closure to witness the engagement, which in a short time, resolved itself into a burlesque. War- rington opened hostilities and the visitors at once pressed. A scrimmage took place near the line and the Hornets were granted a free kick, the attempt at goal failing. From a rush the visitors scored under the posts, the try not being improved upon. The Rochdale men continued to have matters all their Own way and half time arrived with the score :—Rochdale two goals, two tries (ten points), Warrington, nil. On , Resuming, Warrington were granted a free kick, Knight falling at goal. The homesters pressed and Antrobus got over. Finalaßicledale thirteen points, Warrington three points.

OTHER MATCHES. Warrington swamp Manningham. This friendly match was played at Manniagham On New Year's Day. Never has the superiority of Lanca- shire football over that of Yorkshire been more truly emphasized than during the course of the present season, for the victory of Lancashire over the Tykes in the county engagement at Rochdale has been followed by a Icing sequence of decisive club victories. The War- ringtoaiana succeeded in starting the new year well by thoroughly routiog the burly Tykes to the tune of 24 points to three. The visitors had their best available team, Fish being the only notable absentee, and Man- ' ningham also were well represented only,Needham being absent. There would be 5,000 present when the game was stated, and after the usual preliminaries the visitors got near Manningham's goal-line, and short sharp passing saw Isherwood score in the first minute. This was followed by another hot attack, and a splendid round of passing between the half-back and three-quarters cul- minated in Elockenhall racing in behind the posts, for 'lsherwood to place a goal. The home forwards applied themselves steadily to the task of beating back their opponents, and by SUCCESSIVe bursts,in which Wilkinson Donaldson, and Cockerham were prominent, they re- moved the scene of hostilities to the near vicinity of the Warrington goal line. They had to concede ground before the more combined efforts of the Warringtonians, who,by a bout of pretty passing, again pressed. Barker came away at the head of a forward relief movement, and then Leo initiated passing in which Moon and Hammond gained ground, and Be water pluckily endeavoured to get farther than • few yards into the visitors' half. Lee made a dash for the line when the home pack had again done well, but he was held out. Then, from a kick out by Greenwood, the ball weric among both sets of for- wards, and the neat scrimmage was followed by fine passing and a repass of Bate, Burgess Liherwood, and Harris, the home team being obliged et) give way to their ?.5. The home halves got the ball out smartly,and ground was gained, but the visit die were too strong in the succeeding play to be resisted, and from a pass by Taylor the ball was touched down for a try by Dicken- son, although this player was keenly tack:el on the line. Isherwood had an easy task in adding another goal. Play was taken to the home half,where Hammond was prompt ly held when he sought to get away. Burgess had to re- tire hurt,and during his absence, the home pack, led by Cockerharn, Barker, and Wilkinson went oh grandly to the Warrington line. Again the retaliating play was profitable, and from a magnificent run and pass by Hallam, near centre, Hockenhall had a clear run in, and a second try was gained by the wing man. Isherwood added a goal. When half time arrived the home side were at a disadvantage of three goals and four tries (18 points) to nil. Dickenson returned the re-start, and Hallam was promptly in evidence with a ponderous punt, which negatived a fine kick by Bowater. Dickenson dribbled through, and matters again looked bad for the Tykes until Wallace cleared, and premature work by Eden cost Warrington at least thirty yards. Hookenhall made a capital effort to get through, Lee just nailing him a:', the Centre flag. Burgess got clear, and looked certain to get in, when he was foully tripped. The visitors continued to make headway, and Pemberton, Eden. and Cunningham were foremost in brilliant work, which endangered the home goal. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Wycliffe P.S.A Society. This society's meeting on Sunday afternoon suffered In attendance owing to continuous rain. Mr. A. AS TO MOVING ABOUT--

" If I moved about much," said Lizzie Dickinson, " I could hardly get my breath." Now if she had been a typewriter or a seamstress this wouldn't have been so bad, as—save in acute asthma— the breathing goes on fairly well if one can only keep quiet. But:Miss Dickinsln was in service, and how in I Mercy's name was she to keep quiet and at the same time keep her Waco ? Ne ; it is upstairs, downstairs, and in my lady's chamber; early to rise and late to bed ,and a perfect raffle of work between whiles. That's how it is.

So we see that she just had to "move about much." although she was really and truly not able to do it. The end was that she apt so ill that threo years 37,0 —at this date.—she was obliged to drop everything and leave her situation:

In her own little 'aceount of her trouble, written about the let of D ic,mber, 1399, the young girl says she had a dry cough that shook her whole body, and a fearful sharp pain in her right side. Her tough was of the kind that frightens young people—and naturally ; these coughs sound so like consumption, and there is plenty of that in dlar old England.

"My appetite," she says, " was about gone. I stood in the greatest need of nourishment, yet when I looked at my meals, or even thought of them, I turned away with a strange feeling of dislike and disgust. " Going on this way, and trying to do Illy work, I got so miserable and weak I didn't know what was to be- come of me. sometimes I felt so wretched and nervous that I lay down On tn sof t and cried like a heart- broken child. " The doctors tried to help me, and of course I wanted ttiem to succeed. d me for over four months, and I saw several others afterwards ; but I kept getting worse in spite of all they could do. " Finally, I commmoel to f tiny I WAS doomed to die, seeing that no good came of the medicines I had used— and thero were so many of them. Who it was that wanted me to try Mother Stigel's Syrup is of no conse- quence to you, evcr: if could remember. Somebody did, however,aud, as I could think of nothing else to do, I did try it. Tao first few dosos made me feel better. I could eat a trifle, and no pain after it. Then my meals began to taste go: d, aol I got etrength out of them. It wasn't long before tin pain want a way,and my breath- ing was much more natural and easy. and I could move about and turn my hand to a bit of work. "In this manner it was that I went ...n improving, and when I had taken four small bottles, of Mother Seigel's Syrup I was quite myself a,glin,and went back to service." A common friend whosaw Miss Dickinson recently, at Askley,near Tetbury, Glos., reports that she is in fine health, and has plenty of colour. Lang may she remain well and happy.

HOCKEY.

Stockton Heath "A" v. Timperley "A."

At Warriagton oh Saturday. Timperley hail rather a weak team, and the Heathens played four first team men. The first half was very even, Stockton Heath having slightly the best of matters, but were unable to penetrate the defence. THE IRON AND STEEL TRADE IN 1900.

The hematite pig iron trade of the North-west of England has been very busily employed throughout the year ; indeed, the demand has been more steadily main- tained and prices have been better upheld than pro). ably during any year in the trade's. history. The year opened under very promising conditions. Prices were at 73s 31 per ton net cash, there was plenty of demand and good prospects, prices were comparatively high and were likely to advance, and the output was well main— tained, with 47 furnances in blast, compared with 40 at the corresponding time in the previous year. Much I satisfaction was felt at the beginnine of the yeer that stocks, which had stood at over 300,000 tons, had bees reduced to 198,847 tons; By the middle of January prices had advanced to 74s per ton, while in the third week in January they were at 77s 41, and at the end of the month at 753 614. During the month stocks had been further reduced by 15,677 tons. The demand was well maintained throughout February, and as the end of that month prices had advanced to 773 311, while makers' iron was as high as 80e. The output of iron was reduced in March, four furnaces being blown out. This was owing to scarcity in the supply of iron Ore from native mines and the dearness of coke, The con- sequence of this shrinkage in output was that stocks . were reduced to 133, 158 tone. In April prices went up with a rush to 863 op in the middle of the month, makers' quotations ranging-from 86s to 905.. The posi- tion of the trade was strengthened during this month by a further reduction in stocks to 116,766 tons. May, found makers very largely sold forward, with prices i varying from 85s to 873 61 per ton n the early days and 80s lid at the close of the month, the furnaces in blast being 45, and the decrease in stocks having been made to the extent of 104,092 tons since the beginning of the year. A stiffer tone was displayed during June, and at the end of the month warrant sellers were at 85e per ton, stocks having further dwindled down to 61,411 tons, Prices averaged about 83s ad durins July, and stocks were brought down to 58,243 tons, while in August orices were at 83i and stocks further reduced to 55,060 tons. September showed a much easier tone, and warrants came down to 793 61 net cash, stocks to 42,529 tons, and 41 fur- naces only were in blast. In the middle of October warrant iron was 75: 101, and at the end of the month at 763 41. At the latter time 40 furnaces were in blast. In November, owing to a strike at the Asaam works, two furnaces were blown out, and from that time to the end of the year only 37 furnaces were in blast, and of these two were engaged in manufacturing spiegeleisen. This reduction, in make naturally led to further reductions in warrant stocks. They have been depleted since the beginning of the year by no use than 473,984 tons, and the stocks now held represent 23,863 tons only. But during November and December there has been a falling off in the hematite market, owing, on the one hand, to the smaller requirements of consumers who are ens,,agel in the steel trade, aid owing , to the gravity of the situation caused by the

dearness and the scarcity of coal. Many smelters found the position robbed them of nearly all their profits. lerice3 gradually eased down from November uo to the last week, when warrant iron was quoted at 661 100, net cash sellers, 663 'lid buyers, and makers quoted 70S. ta 723 6d per ton. Although, however, fewer furnaces have been in blaet during the year than during 1899, it is not certain but that the output of pig iron will be equally large, as more tonnage is being produced by the furnaces, under heavier blast, and many of the furnaces having been increased in capacity. Several furnaces on the west coast now preduce 1,000 tons of pig iron per week. It may be roughly estimated that the pro- duce of west; coast furnaces this year will total up to 2,000,000 tens. The native iron ore trade has been very active. The demand has been full and steady thronshout. indeed, , the requirements of local and other smelters—for a con- siderable tonnage of native ore is carried by rail and sea to Sheffield, the Midlands, South Wales, Scotland, and elsewhere—could not b 3 met, and it is safe to say Met at any rate 45 per cent of tee ore smelted on the west coast during the year has been drawn from ' Spanish and other foreign mines. Native iron ore has ruled at from 153 per ton in January to 17a per toa in December, net at mines for good average sorts, while Robio ores have ranged from 163 61 to 193 per ton, and are now at 183 delivered at west coast ports. A vey fine and highly marketable type of iron is produced from native iron ores and Spanish ores mixed. Active search has been made during the year for new encl 3 of native ore,and in several cases good pockets of metal have been discovered There are evidences, however, of a falling off in the iron ore mining industry, unless some very important finds are made soon. The steel trade haswhh beenthe pt exception yfiuolhly employed through- out the year, it of the months of November and December, and the probability is the there would have been uninterrupted activity through- out the year had it not been fee the prejudicial influence exerted on the trade by high-ericed coal. The extra cost of this item led to the naaiutenance of high prices for plates, rails, and other classes of produce too long, , until in fact, the ring of Wed makers found themselves suddenly brought f ice to face with American competi- tion. Almost before they were aware of it, American makers had booked large orders for rails, plates &a., at much below British combination prices. The year opened with rails at £7 and with plates at ZS. By June prices had gone up to £7 73 61 for rails and £8 7‘i 61 for plates, boiler plates being at £9 153. In September rails were down at £7 55 and pieties at £8 ss, and in ()etcher they were again lower at £7 for rails and £8 for pieties, Teen in November prices reached £6 103 for heavy rails and £6 17s 61 for plates. Since then new beeinees has beia scarce, and th 3 mills have been in- differently employed. During tha year there has been much activity in hoop, billets, tin bars, slabs, merchant steel, and heavy steel ceetings, and orders are still well held for hoop 3, billete, and merchant steel. There are geed prosele,ts for the steel trade in the spring of next ?Tar.
