EARTH'S DEE PEST HOLES.

In the days when people really thought it was possible to dig a hole through the earth to Australia, there used to be much speculatioa over what would become of a man who happened to fall into the hole:; would he stop falling after a while and begin to fall up to the other side of the earth, or would gravity ken him oscillating , like a pendulum slowly coming to rest? Now nonsensi- cal as this ma; seem, we are actually approaching a con- dition when a somewhat similar qeestion must he asked Several holei have been due deep enough into the earth to encounter physical conditions very different from those usually found on the surface. Some of these holes are in the form of wells which are a mile deep. Others extend even farther into salt beds and still others are mines which men work at a level as deep s s that of the wells. And in the ocean bed there are holes so deep that if the highest mountains in the world were to be dropped iota them there would still be plenty of room for ships to sail safely over their summits. At Paruschowilz, near Reihnik, in &stern Silesia, there is a well which is now 6,700 feet deep, or exactly 100 feet more than a miie and a quarter. Tney are atilt boring, and it is the intention to go down 8,800 feet, or one and two-thirds miles, when some interesting scientific experiments will be made. Unquestionably this is now the deepest artificial hole in the world. At the bottom of that five-mile pit in the ocean which yawns between the Kermadecs and tbe Friendly Islands the temperature stays always near the freezing pnint of water. There are many of these deep ocean holes in which the temperature is very low. And while the lack of heat is induced by the water and its pressure, it yet bears a certain relation to the condition of deep holes in the continents when the general effect on the earth's crust is considered. For when the theory of the earth's internal heat is look id at superficially it would seem that these deep sea pits should be very hot and full of boiling water, since they are nearer to the centre of the earth teen any other point. But in this matter of temperature there is much to be said concerning the conducting properties of the rock in which the holes are sunk. Also, it must be noted at what depth below sea level each hole starts. As wells all get hotter and hotter as they are driven deeper and deeper, the outcome suggested is, that as soon as a sufficient depth, Is reached, natural steam will be encountered, or if the well be dry, water can be Pumped in and returned in the form of marketable steam. There is nothing chimerical in this idea, since many buildings in several parts of the world are heated with naturally warm well-water. The hospital at Gre- nelle and large factories in Wurtemberg are notable examples. Also the geyser shows bow heat from the earth's interior may manifest itself forcibly on the sur- face. Hence temperature messurements are being made in all of the wells as they are drilled. The heat increases on the average about one degree for every 60 feet of depth. _ _ _ _ _ .

Th method of taking these temperatures is simple. Self-registering thermometers were placed in iron buc- kets 3 feet long and 3 inches in diameter. A bucket hold- ing thermometers was hung on the end of a steel wire and let down into the hole 260 feet,when another bucket was tied on and the lowering was continued. Measurements were thus made at various depths. it was tedious work, and when the lower depths were reached it required nearly an hour to get the buckets back to the surface once the measurements had been made. On the way down in the Pittsburg well,natural gas was encountered, tapped and used for a time to drive the machinery that worked the drills. In this instance the well furnished power to dig itself out. The heat increases more rapidly as the hole gets deeper. But these wells were all bored in what might be called ' the normal crust of the earth, where the strata lie in situ. Had they been dug in Wyoming, in the Geyser Basin, for instance, it is probable that steam or very hot water would have been eccountered not many feet below the surface, for after all, the geyser is one of nature's deepest holes, since it connects in one way or another with the internal heat of the earth and, therefore, we have a sort of indication of how an artificial deep hole might act if once it were driven down far enough to form a vent for the heat of the earth. On the other hand, had one of the wells been driven down under Michigan, through the copper beds, it is probable that even 10,000 feet would not beget a temperature too hot for breathing purposes, lot alone the production of steam. This must appear strange in view of the fact that the region is full of copper-bearing rock, anti therefore ought to conduct heat readily. Nevertheless, it is a fact that these rocks do not conduct the heat, for the, proximity of the waters of Lake Superior has a cooling effect on the copper mines, Low temperatures are characteristic of all the suba- queous deep holes. Beside the chasms in the ocean bed the holes in the land are as pin punctures, for more than hill of the whole sea floor lies two miles below the surface of the water. One eighth of this latter area is depressed below three miles. This eighth. itself is 7,000.000 square geographical miles in extent, and con- tains areas, or, rather, basins. These almost unfathoin. able holes in the ocean bottom occur only in three places or, at least, only three soundings of five miles or more have been made. There may be ethers, of course, even deeper, which have, as yet remained undiscovered. The deepest ef these holes exists in the South Pacific, to the east of the Kermadecs. It is 5155 fathoms deep, or 530 feet more than five geographical miles. The sounding that went to its bottom represents the farthest reach of human-kind toward the centre of the earth. Yet deep as the " Aldrich " is, in spite of the fact that Mount Everest, the highest mountain in the world, if dropped into the hole, would sink out of sight in the ocean, that little pinnacle called Sunday Island, stand- ing squarely in this five-mile hole, is able to rear its head 2,000 feet above the surface of the sea. Incidentally this conveys a vivid idea of the contrast nature is able to make in the matter of high hills and deep holes. And that introduces us, naturally enough, to the subj,L•ct of our next article. " Science Siftings."

Another Fatal Fall at Warrington.

An inquiry into the circumstances attending the death of Richard Wicherley, sixty years of age, who resided at 10. Back Stamford-street, was conducted at Oa Police Station on Monday evening by the County Coroner (Mr S Brighouse). From the evi- dence it appeared that at 7 o'clock on the evening of Christmas Day the'deceased went to the house of his son,who lives at al, West-street to see his wife who was ill in bed. He went upstairs and the son went out of the house. On returning at 9 o'clock he heard a noise on the stairs, as if someone was following. He at once went to the foot of the stairs and found the deceased lying there. Dr. Adams was sum- moned an,i attended to the deceased injuries and on the following day he was removed to the Infirmary where he died on the 2.0-1 amember, trotn the effects of a fractured spine. Previous to going to the Infirmary deceased told his son that he was leaving the bedroom when he missed his footing on the landing and fell downstairs. Deceased was very short sighted—The Jury returned a verdict of "Accidental Death."

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

Wise men make proverbs for fools to follow. A DIAN is known by the photographs he keeps. Tun average man thinks he is above the average. NATIIIIAL TO Asa.—" They say he is a natural born genius." "Who supports him? ' A NAN is as old as he feels ; a woman as old as she tells. A CYNIC is a man who will laugh at anything so long as it isn't humorous. 14 LIKE CASE. —A : "I don't mind the influenza itseli—it's the after-effect I'm afraid of." B: The after-effect is what ails me. I'm dodging the doctor for two guineas at the present time. "WHAT was Nero's great crime?" asked the teacher of a history class. "He played the fiddle," replied Sammy Wiffies. MAN (to angler): "Have you caught anything, gudnor ?" Angler: "Of course not l I'm not a fisherman; I'm an angler." JACK (tenderly, to the little brother of his adored one) : "Would you like to know a secret, Tommy ? " Tommy: "Should think I would." sack: "Well, I'm in love with your sister." Tommy: "Oh, that's no secret. The family has talked about it every day since Aunt Emma promised Nell that she'd bring about an introduc- tion."

BRIDE (throwing her arms about the bridegroom's neck) : "You are my prisoner for life." Bride- groom: "It's not imprisonment for life, love; it's capital punishment.' A BRoAD HlNT.—Visitor : "Is Miss Rose at home?" Servant : "No, sir." Visitor: " Why, she has just come in! I saw her." Servant : l° Yes, sir; and she saw you, too." San: "You ought to be ashamed of yourself, John, for shooting such a dear little bird." He: "1 thoeght you would like it for your hat."—She: "Oh, what a Food idea I That was very thoughtful of you, John. ' HUSBAND: "It seems to me that you eorne to my office a good deal more than there is any necessity for." Wife: "I cannot help it dear; your manners in the office are so much nicer than they are at home, that I like to enjoy the contrast." " WHy did you break off your engagement with Miss Bertha?" "Because her parrot was always saying, Stop that, George !" "But what differ- ence did that make? Your engagement was not a secret." "But my name is not George." PROVED.—" No, said Mr. Rockwell, as he wiped his glasses, "I'm afraid John's college education isn't going to do him much good after all." "Why, William," his anxious wife cried, "what makes you say that?" "He admitted in the shop yesterday that there were still a few things I knew more about than he did." UNCLE EDEN'S PIIILOSoPHY.—" Dar am' no good o' being backward in die life," said Uncle Eben. 6' De parrot ain't a very smaht bird, but it manages purty comfortable jes' by gittin' on de perch an' hollerin' de little bit it knows." IN DAYS OF OLD.—The official bulletin of the War Department read as fellows: "A dispatch has just been rec6ved from C:esar. He says, Veni, vidi, vici.'" The Roman people were plainly annoyed. "Why in Tartarus doesn't he send particulars?" they asked in one voice. AN UNLOOKED-FOR TlFF.—She : "Did you say they had a tiff and the engagement was broken off?" He: "Yes, but the tiff came after it was broken off. In fact, she was it—the plain-tiff in the action for breach of promise." Mns. A TEACHER asked a little boy to spell "respon- sibility," which he did. "Now, Tommy, said the teacher, "can you tell me the meaning of that big word?" " Yis, mum," answered Tommy. "If I had only four buttons on my trousers, and two came off, all the responsibility would hang on the other two." GROWN-UP WOMEN.—" Clara," said the mother of a litle five-year-old miss, who was entertaining a couple of neighbouring girls of her own age, "why don't you play something instead of sitting still and looking miserable ? " "Why, mamma, we is playing'," was the reply; " we's playin' that we's growed-up woman." HAD To TELL SOME ONE.—" If you'd been half an hour later," she said, "I don't know what I should have done." HE AGREED.—" Much time that might be better employed," said the advocate of spelling reform, "is taken up in the writing of foolish and unneces- sary latter." "It is indeed," was the earnest comment of the young man who had a breach of promise suit hanging over his future like a damp and dismal cloud. AFTER THAT.—A well • known university pro- fessor was lecturing to some students on hygiene some time ago, when one of the class—from the provinces—asked him how he—the student— could safeguard himself in drinking London water. The professor rather startled him by replying— " First boil it, then filter it, and, after that-- drink beer." WHY Hz GRUMBLED. "About the funniest guest we ever had," said a hotel clerk, was a solemn-looking man who came in late one night and went to his room. An hour later he rushed back into the office as mad as a hornet. "After some time he quieted down enough to tell his troubles. "• I came here,' he declared, 'to com- mit suicide by turning on the gas, and you put me in a room lighted by electricity " HE (after a quarrel, much affected) : "And do —do you really mean that we must part, Maud ? "—She: Nev. never to meet again I "—He: But—but suppose we should inset again by accident ?"—She " I—l never thought of tha-that. It is just pos-possible. Goo- good-bye, darling I "—She: "Goo-good-bye for— ever, Ge-George I" SUITING BOTH PARTIE.9.—Poor Wife (to husband, whose loud snoring keeps her awake): "Charlie, Charlie, do stop snoring. Turn over on your aide." (Nudges him). Husband, only half awake, grunts, turns on his side, and continues to snore. Wife has a happy idea. Remembers a line from an article called "How to Prevent Snoring." Gives her husband a second nudge, which elicits another grunt. "Oh, Charlie, if you'd keep your mouth shut you'd be all right." Charlie (semi-conscious): "So would you." MARRIAGE A LOTTERY. —He looked happy enough as he walked up to the post-office box, set a huge bundle on the floor, and began taking pretty square envelopes therefrom, dropping them by two and threes in the box. " Big lot of letters," remarked the policeman. " Nice day, too." " Letters !" said the happy man. "My dear fel- low, these are not letters ; they are wedding invi— tations." A stern look came over the face of the hitherto friendly policeman. "My friend," he said, "I am sorry to disturb you, but I must do my duty. Come with me." "Arrested ?" " Yes." "On what charge? This is an outrage." " Nob at all. You are advertising a lottery through the post. BETTING ON' A CERTAINTY.—A lieutenant was shooting on the rifle range, and he " called " each shot as he fired, without waiting for the markers to signal the result. "You're a pretty good guesser," said the colonel. "Why don't you admit you're guessing where those shots land?" "I'll bet you a box of cigars," said the junior officer, ::that I can call twenty shots in succession." Taken,, said the old warrior. The lieutenant

"Miss," he announced, and a red flag from the target told that this was correct. Another shot. "Miss," he declared. A third shot. "Miss again," he said. Fourth shot. "Fourth miss," he announced. Another shot. "Miss," again sang out the lieutenant. "Hold on there I" put in the colonel. "What are you trying to do? I thought you were going to fire at the target." "I'm trying to win my box of cigars," said the lieutenant. "Don't fire any more," said the colonel. "They're yours." Lococsra Puustosiro WAFERE—More cures of Bronchi- tie VOICE. latters to the Obttor.

_ Dr. Peacocke and the Guardians.

Sir,—On reading the account of the Warrington Board of Guardians' procedings in your issue of Satur- day last, I was surprised to find the Guardians were under the impression that my absence from the Board- room was caused by some imaginary appointment else- where. I wish to state that on the morning the Board met I had aa important business engagement. I went to inform Mr Sutton (the clerk), of the fact ; and further I made a special visit to meet Mr A J Jolley (the chairman), and explained to him the nature of my business engagement. as I felt it seas due to the chairman, that he should know why I could not be present at the meeting, and I quite expected he would have mentioned this fact to the Board. My solo object in writing this letter is that the Guar , dians and public may know,that the chairman although silent as to the cause of my absence, was aware of the important nature of my business, Yours etc., WILLIAM J. FEACOCKE. 95, Butterrnarket-street, Warrington. December 27th, 1900. The Political Arrangement re Municipal Elections.

Sir.—Many people were of the opinion that the arrangement between the Liberals and Conservatives ot Warrington not to oppose the nominees of one another for the Town Council was made ica order to avoid the necessity and trouble of an election and thereby induce the best and most capable men to give their services to the town. It is rather interesting to learn now that the whole affair is a dodge planaed to suit a certain cliqu consiating of members of both parties whose chief,if no only aim, is their own individual gain. Whichever be the real motive for such an arrangement the doing of the thing reflects little credit on the electors of Warrington. The first indicates plainly enough that Democratic government in Warrington is a failure, that the electors are intellectually and morally unfit for the duties of citizenship, that they are hopelessly incapable of distinguishing the good from the bad, the true from false. If the second be true the condition of things is made much worse—if worse be possible. By allowing such a thing it at once shows the depth of the apathy and ignor- ance of the electorate. One need not therefore wonder at socue men thinking the morale taf the Council could best be improved by a scheme of disenfranchisement. and electing the members to the Council by invitation— at all times a dangerous expedient, but perhaps neces-

sary. 1t cannot be denied that so long as mem- bers of the Council were elected by ballot, pro- gress in Warrington remained a dream, never at any time _partaking of the nature of a reality or ever likely to do so. The great drawback to the disenfranchisement idea is that any improvement can only be temporary, at the same time toeing attended with great dangets. Better far to have an intelligent and interested electorate. It only can be the basis of ' any real progress. The best men of the town while refusing to aerie the suffrage of ignoranouses will hav3 no scruples to come before men able to understand and appreciate them. The friends of Voluntary 6,3h001s never cease to pro- claim that the superiority of Voluntary over Board School is that they impart to the young a high moral and religious tone, that they create a race of men of intelligence, character and integrity, that they endow man with the basis of all that goes to the formation of a good citizen. Tney in fact profess to do all that Warrington lacks and Warrington never haa any but Voluntary schools. War- rington has been educated by Voluntary schools with a high moral and religious tone as their strong points, and both political parties have decreed that it is unfit to elect members to its Town Council. Something is very far wrong, at least that is the opin- ion of a SCOT. December 26th, 1900.

The Late Trooper Swann. Memorial Service at Newton.

Owing to the great esteem in which Trooper Swann was held by his comrades at home a meting of members of the' C " troop of Lancashire Hussars wan held last week, at which it was decided to reenmmend the hold- ing of a memorial service and parade of the squadron on Sunday. The idea was eagerly taken up,end permission having been obtain.d from Lord Newton, notices were issued cot only to the troop, but to the other Voluntspr companies in Newton. The response was entirely sotis- factory, and the necessary arrangemen's haying, been made with the Rev. J H Ryder, vicar of Newton, the parade was decided upon. The morning opened wet and stormy, and there were gloomy forebodings, which were realized by the rain falling in a steady downpour all the morning. The muster was for 1015, and the parade at 10-30, and • about this time something like 60 hussars and a goodly number of the Volunteer com- panies of the 6th V.B.K.L R., and the let 8 L Regiment put in an appearance. The hussars were without arms, but the Volunteers wore side arms, and all marched from near the Oak Tree Hotel to St Petet'a Church, the route being lined by a large number of sympathetic sperteors, despite the chilling condition of the elements. Tee Church was uncomfortably filled,and much difficulty was experi- enced in providing seats for those wishing to be present. Most of the relatives of the deceased trooper were pr'- sent, and evinced the greatest interest in the service. The officiating minister was the Rev. James Ryder, 'and the usual:morning serviceiwas gone through, the follow- ing appropriate hymns being impressively sung, the con- gregation joining heartily :—" Thy will be done," " Holy Father in Thy mercy," " Onward, Christian Soldiers," and " The voice of God's creation found me." For his sermon the Vicar took for his text I, Corinthians. 15 chapter and part of 54 verse—" Death, is swallowed up in victory." Death he said, had gone through hell and had found no fire, and had gone through Christ and had found no resurrection. He after- wards observed that they had that morning to think of one from amongst themselves, who had been highly re- pected, nay, he might say, affeeionately loved by his fellow townsmen. One whom, he believed had said that rather than return home maimed, he would like his life taken away. He;wanted to come back perfect or not at all. ei.Whilst they thought of death, they should remem- ber that the Gospel taught them how frail they were and how near death was to every one of them. Re crieted the Apostle Paul, where he states that this life was only the beginning of a happier and better rlife, and that we only meet to part again. He asked them to think what it was that had sustained the Commander in Chief in South Africa, Lord Roberts. in these awful days, and to think of what now remained of his only son. What was it, he wked, that bad made him so cheerful through all these trying times ?

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS

The usual fortnightly meeting of the above Board was held at the Workhouse, Dutton, on Tuisday last. Mr Joseph Stokes presided, and there were also present : —Mr A R Norman. J Moa lowerpft, thl Rural District Councillors, Mr G F Ashton (clerk), and Mr W E Hough assistant clerk).

A HAPPY NEW YEAR. The Chairman, in a wry short hut choice address, wished the members of the Board a happy New Year. Toe occasion upon which they met that day was unique to them ; for besides being the first day of the year. it was the first day of the nentury. In the past, he thought the business of the Bard had been conducted harmoniously, and hoped it would continue so in the future. Ele was sorry that the Chairman (Mr J Moseley Williams) wag absent, for he could have referred more fully to a retrospect of the past year. During that time they had lost two members they all respected very much—Mr Weir. of Walton, who was the oldest member of the Board, and Mr W Pickering, of Norley. In conclusion he hoped that a feeling of love and good fellowship would continue to actuate them in the discharge of their duty to both rich and poor. LETTER PRIM SIR J. T. BRUNNER, APP. THE PURE BEER BILL The following correspondence was read by the Clerk, received in reoly to tha resolution of the Board of Guardians requesting the local memb.ra of Parliament to support Sir Cuthbert Quitter's Pure Beer Bill. Druids Cross Wavertree, Liverpool, December 20 h, I9oo.—Dear Sir.—Sir John Brunner wishes me to ac- knowledge the receipt of your letter of yesterday,and to say that he thinks he oas,ht to draw your attention to the letter in yesterday's " Times " from Mr C Esteourt, analyst to the city of Manchester in which he "says that he has found arsenic in malt in quite sufficient quantity to show in the beer which is Produced from it as much arsenic as has been found in beer brewed from arsenical glucose. This discov 'Tv clearly proved that Sir Cuthbert Quilter'e Pure Beer Bill will not cure the evil, and that the whole subjct requires further careful consideration. Sr John wiatiee me to add that the real cure in his opinion is to make the brewers directly and immediately responsible for the quality of their beer, and to give the publicans complete trcedom to buy their beer whs.:re they choose, a freedom of which they are wholly depeived when the houses are tied. Yours faithfully, W. H. Young. George F. Ashton Esq. Mr R 1 Daasey, poor law inspector of the Local Government Board, referred to a circular issued by the Local Government Board which recommended for one thing that the Guardians form a special class of aged, dessrving inmates, and grant them special prisileges He had drawn the attention of the House Committee to the matter, and they had promised to consider the question in conjunction with the Master. He told them this, so they would not be suprised when a report was submitted.—The circular also recommended the Guardians to grant out-relief to aged deserving poor which, should be adequate in amouar„ That was, when I they found some very deserviag aged poor, they should not confine themselves to granting out- relief to the extent of the usual her-crown • bat give them something which would make them independent of charity. (Hear, hear.) He would like 0 know what answer the Clerk could give as to the position of the Board in that matter.—Another question, too, was the suggestion of removing children out of the house. But, of course, they in Runcorn were not in a position to do more than the sending of their children to an outside school at present. The Clerk intimated he would be in a position to reply to the Local Government Board ia the matter of the question of granting out-relief to the aged deserving poor, that their payments were quite equal, if not greater, than those granted by other Boards, and that the Guardians would Rive special attention to deserving cases brought to their notice. Mr. T. Wright added he, too, c,ould answer the ques- tion for so far as several cases he had been interested in. instead of granting the usual half-crown, they had granted 4s. That was subsequent to the receipt of the circular. (Hear, hear.) Mr Dansey added that he had been " round the house," and had a good look at the children. He was Flad to see that the Board had got their excellent indue- trial trainer (Mr Roberts) back again, and could con- gratulate them upon the state of their children, who were clean and well looked arter. They would have gomething before them, on the question of nurses in their House Doctor's next report.

ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL. _ LANCASHIRE LEAGUE.

Earlestown v. Nelson.

After a hard holiday programme, Earlestown were at home on Saturday to receive Nelson. The week previeus they paid a visit to Nelson and returned defeated by three goals to one, but they amply avenged this defeat on Saturday. The home team underwent several changes from that which defeated Hayclack on Boxing Day, Green and Anthony being dropped, Hayes and Clibborn replacing them. The visitors were at full strength, their team being identically the same as that which defeated Earlostown the previous week. Nelson won the toss and elected to play with the hill, Brown starting before about 400 spectators. A brief visit was immediately paid to the visitors' goal but Little ran the ball over the line. The Nelson forwards instituted a nice combined movement on the home citadel and Cook- son gave CrieW a warm handful. Bartley next got going on the home right but ha passed weakly and the leather was very soon again in the home half The visitors forwards were, however, in,ff ective when ia the vicinity of the goal and were easily kept at bay by the backs. Settle was getting nicely away when he was foully floored by Almon and the free kick led to a fierce onslaught on the Nelson goal, Bartley relieving the pressure by shooting wind. Shortly afterwards the visitors goal narrowly missed capture, Settle getting away nicely and after tricking Howarth, abet hard in, the ball striking the bottom of the upright and rebound- ing in touch. Still keeping up the pressure, Bartley skimmed the bar from long range aad later B,rearn shot sp'endidly the ball sticking in the mud and stopping dead almost on the geal line, the cus:odian just getting it away before he was bundled o'er. At this period the game became rather rough and as a conse- quence Howarth was injured and the visitors played one back thus throwing the Earleatown forwards offside. Just before the interval Bartley received a pas) from Lee and breaking through, shot her 1 and trite for goal. Whittaker put his foot up to stop the leather, butTrown caught it on the rebound and had no diffleulty in draw- ing first blood on behalf of Earlestown. Half-time Earlest own one, Nelson nil. In the sec-rid portion, the homesters had matters prs.etica'ly all their own way and before the finish!Brown put on two more goala and Settle one, so that Earles- town came out with flying colours, the final score being four to nil in their favour.

The return match betwoPn those two local rivals was played at flavdonk on Nsw Year's Day. Earlestown plavel the same team that performed so well ag,ainst Nelson whilst Haydoek were at full strength. THE GALE.

Effects of the Gale at Widnes.

The effects of the recent gale at Widnes were not alartniogly serious. In fact, little damage was done to house property with the exception of a public-house windows being blown in, and a few chimney pots and slates dislodged from various buildings. Scene on the River Mersey. A Warrington Boat in Danger. What would originally have been described as" Run- corn Gap." presented a rough aspect during the vale on Friday and Saturday. Luckily, however, the craft of Runcorn and Widnes had for the greater part been snugly harboured. The tug " Dagmar " of Runcorn, however, came in for an exciting adventure. Whilst boating up the river (light) a Warrington fishing boat was noticed in difficulties and signalling distress. The tug straightway put off to her aid and eventually took her in tow. The boat was, however, in too dangerous a condition to be ventured far with, and the captain thought it advisable to tow it on to a sand-bank by Widnes. He rescued the crew (three men) and on account of the serious condition of exhaustion in which he found one of thms Is.nded them at Widnes. The men were all of Warrington. The fishing smack was the " Explorer "of Warrington belonging to Mr J Sherratt, Faircloug,h's-avenue, War- rington. The arrival of the Dagmar, and its plucky Captain (T Smith, of Runcorn), was very timely as one of the men was in an almost comatose condition due to his long exposure in the bitter cold rainstorm. Damage at Runcorn.

Friday's gale did great damage in Runcorn. In every part of the town slates and chimney-pots were dislodged, while in more exposed situation roofs were ripped off and window-frames blown bodily in. Many atreets were strewn with wreckave. Fortunately no personal accidents were recorded. Exciting scenes were witnessed on the Merrier. the wind blowing with terrific force during the tide and before it, and the water rose about six feet over the normal height, and swept over the Ship Canal in great volumes. At Weston Point, the dockmen could not stand on the pierhead. Traffic on the river and Ship Canal was practically at a standst ill. The Manchester Citv, from Manchester to Halifax, Canada, tied up at Runcorn at noon, and did not pro- ceed until ter; on Saturday morning. The traffic on the Bridgewater Canal was practically all" tied up "during the progress of the gale, but nothing beyond a little in- convenience was experienced along this important water way.

Vessel Damaged in the Ship Canal. The so. Ariarine, of Homburg, collided on Friday with Mode Wheel 1.1a4, in the Manchostor stijo cam!. receiving &maga to the bow. She has gone into the graving dock. The Storm at Earlestown and Newton.

IThe violent storm of wind and rain which raged over the district on Friday last, fortunately did very little damage, and there were no accidents to human life. Slates in several parts were blown from houses, and in Boundary-street, a fence was torn off, and driven across the street, and in Wargrave-road a fence round some Iflew buildings was thrown down. In Crossley-stree,the front windows which are in an exposed position were blown in, and in Regent•street the chimney stack of a house was blown down, and the pavement was strewn with bricks, but fortunately none penetrated the roof of the house, and nobody w^s injured. NOTES ON UAMES AND

ATHLETICS. Rugby Football.

[By " Ovalist "] This week I feel buuud to change my tune and sing the praises of the Warrington team, for they not only finished the century with a creditable win, but they also won their first match of the new century in handsome fashion. This is better than was expected. and one must certainly give credit where credit is due. [ Rochdale Hornets were the opponents of War- rington in the concluding match of the century, and as they wri pitying ou their own ground the odds were largely in their favour, but it was they instead of Warrington who failed to rise to the occasion. Warrington did not repeat their perfor- mance earlier in the seaaon when they defeated the Hor- nets by a large margin, lant they got the two points and that is the main thing—espec:ally as they were very badly wanted.

It is not to Dane-street that the Roclidalian goes for Rugby football now--day. That horriblo swamp has been at last deserted by the Flornets Club which has been fortunate enough to se :we the playing pktch at the Athletic Grounds for its matches. Here there 13 a good turf and ample acc mu:iodation for both spec- tators and players, and Warrington ought to consider it a happy augury that their first visit to the Athletic Grounds has resulted in victory.

The handful of spectators who went to Rochdale were not very much encouraged when they found that the team included about half the " A" team, and that Isher- wood and Fish were among the absentees. Chances of success looked very poor indeed, even though the Hor- nets w era not much better represented, Bit one can never tell how junior players will acquit themselves. The Warrington lads gave quite a surprising display and played just the right game under the circumstances. They deserve every credit for winning the match.

Rochdale played shockingly and on the diiplay they gave tau are quite the worst team I have seen this season. But toe form of Saturday cannot be a true in- dication. From what one cmn learn things do not work very harmoniously in the Rochdale club and some of the , players ate not very particular whether they win or not. Toere ware shirkers among them when they came to Warrington, but they have worsened a lot since then. Burgess displayed capital judgment when he went in for an open game to commence with, and the way he ran n d passed proved what a good half back he really is. Unfortunately he won't play the same gameconsistently. Oa Saturday he repeatedly ran through and gave his centres the ball with the result that Ratcliffe and Harris were within an ace of scoring in the first ten minutes. The combination was really excellent and after Field gave his side the lead with a penalty goal, the Warring- ton men showed their mettle to some purpose. Ratcliffe comdleted a clever movement with a very neat try, and the game was practically won, for Warrington easily held their own.

In the second half the Hornets got a big chance of vic`ory for Scott dislocated his ankle and made the third Warrington 1141 f-back to be disabled in play in a week. Elen took the vacant place and the leaders went in for defensive tactics. Rochdale pressed repeatedly but their method of attack was so crude that Warrington easily kept them out. Judicious marks and long punts were the order and though it was a sorry display from a spectator's point of view, Warrington did the right thing under the circumstances and the thing too, that Rochdale have always done when they got ahead. The Hornets have won fame for gaining victories by marks.

Wightman and Rigg were the only men on the home side who showed any knowledge of the game acd both worked hard. Field was aw NI, and his pules to the wing beggared description. Generally they went nearer Hallam than Wightman, whom they were meant for. And yet Field was preferred to Fisb for County honours! The Warrington msn all shaped very well indeed,though not one was free from occasional clumsiness in fielding the ball. Burgess was the best man on the field, while Rateldfn fully justified his selection. He is a smart lad arecl deserves further trial on a drier day;

Flushed with their victory over Rochdale Hornets, Warrington proceeded on New Year's Day to Valley Parade,Manningharn,and smote that famous team hip sad thigh. The Warrington team was a strong one, though Fish was still absent owing to a severe cold, and R Bate took Scott's place at half back. Manningham also whipped up a full team with the intention of teaching the vilitors that there was football in Yorkshire yet, even if Lancashire were the champion County. From the start. however, Warrington played brilliantly aad quite outclasaed the team which used to administer such " whackings " all round. To say the Tykes were astonished at Warrington's display and ultimate victory by 26 points to three is an entirety inadequate descrip• tion of their feelings. They asked all sorts of questions and seemed generally to imagine that Warrington had whipped up a batch of internationals for their benefit. llockenhall was particularly brilliant on the right wing and raced in three times, while Isherwood, Dickenson and Harris, also credited themselves with tries. It sa as entirely by smart back play that Warrington triumphed and truly the combination of the winners was a revelation. Were it only main- tained week by week the club would not occupy so lowly a position.

The Committee are so pleased with the performance of the men that the same team has been chosen en bloc, and there are even predictions floating abut that Swin- ton will meet with a check on Saturday. I fancy not ! Even my enthusiasm and hopefulness does not encourage me to expect that. Warrington certainly mean to try hard, however, and they were very near the mark last season.

P.S.A.

The usual weekly mealnii--of the Earlestown and District P.S.A. l77ociety, was held in the Co-opera- tive Hall, Earlestown on Sunday afternoon. Mr Williamson presided, and an excellent address on the closing of the year was Riven by Mr Dring. Vocal items were given by Miss Tombs, and Mr S Dring presided at the piano. GOOD TEMPLAR.

In connection with the Hope of Earlestown Lodge, a social was held at the Co-operative Hall, on Monday evening, commencing at 630 with a tea. Owing to the limited accommodation the number had to be curtailed. After tea a short entertain- ment was given, Bro Cutler in the chair, and the following programme was submitted:—Piano solo, " Hilarity," Bro Willis; song," Bells of Aberdovey," Sister Thomas ; reading, "Ben Seddon's Courtship," Bro Warburton: song," Couldn't take the t7iospeorf us," Bro Huddleston ; mouth organ solo," w and I shall hear," Bro Ellison ; song, " I'se dream- ing of you," Sister Miss Gosling; recitation, "An act in the drama of intemperance." Bro Roberts song, "Down the road," Bro Huddleston ; song; "Just a little bit of string," Sister Miss Goslin. The rest of the evening was spent in games, the Orches- tral Band visiting and giving several selections,

Personal.

_ The Rev. Angus Macdonald, curate of St. James', Latchford, preached his farewell sermon on Sunday evening, and left Warrington on Monday for Shef- field, where he has been appointed assistant curate at the Church of St. John the Evangelist. The members ot the Young Men's Bible Class presented him with a handsome umbralla,and he was also the recipient at the hands of other friends of a silver soverign purse filled with soverigns and hall-sovereigns, an um- brella. an ebony walking-stick, and a pipe. The Belvoir meet at Mr. and Mrs. Montague Thorold's picturesque place, Honington Hall, on riday was made the scene of the presentation to Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall of the souvenirs offered to them by the ladies on the Lincolnshire side of the country on the occasion of their wedding, which took place in October. The presents consis- ted of an old silver jag, dating back to 1783, with a stand bearing an appropriate Inscription; and a , diatnon,l and pearl brooch. ' Amongst the gentlemen who qualified as county magistrates at the Chester Quarter Sessions on Mon- day we notice the name of Mr. J. Malin Edelsten, of Quernfield, Orappenhall.

A PEW TIMELY REMARKS BY pIT TS' S, LONDON-ROAD, MANCHESTER (Just past Station, through Railway Arch). ALSO Al PITTS'S PANTHEON' GAYTHORN (Just past Manchester Central Station). THE PROSPEROUS GENERAL FURNISHERS. ONTHE THRESHOLD OF A NEW YEAR, the THE of PITTS'S Business Career, we deem it ad- visable to scan past working in order to draw inspiration , and guidance for the future. As space permits but a brief retrospect, we refer only to what has been achieved since our advent in London-road. PITTS'S aspire and persevere, or, in homely language, we aim high and keep pegging at it. This, we believe, is the only royal road to success, the only path to prosperity, the one bee-line for the ambitious. We pursue it now and always have done, with what results the following shows. First, as regards quality of business, TT MAY INTEREST YOU TO KNOW that we have had the honour of aupplying,from London-road,furniturd for the office of. HIS GRACE THE DUKE OF DEVONSHIRE, KG., and that wa have had the pride and pleaaare of executing, from Gaythorn, two separate orders for HIS MAJESTY THE SULTAN OF MOROCCO. A really magnificent lounge suite specially manufactured by PITTS'S. which formed a portion °tithe latter orders, was exhibited in our Showrooms and gained unstinted praise from those who saw it, whilst it caused unfeigned delight to His Majesty's representatives who had actually ordered the goods. IN ADDITION TO THE BLIT E, we have minis- tered to the requirements of most other grades of society, down to the humble, though necessary, artisan, besides pub- lic and private institutions, corporations, &c.. &a., and in several instances!PlTTS'S HAVE FURNISHED THE THIRD GENERATION, having previously supplied the household requisities for the parents and grandparents when they first embarked in married life, and we hope, in the fulness of time to do the like for the fourth and succeeding generations. NOW AS REGARDS THE QUANTITY OF BUSINESS. The great and instantaneous success ef the Pantheon at Gaythorn, opened in June last, has con- siderably augmented, PITTS'S already vast turnover, caus- ing the sales for the past six months to EXCEED BY A RATE of £20,000 per annum those of the same period in 1896, whilst materially strengthening PITTS'S prestige and enabling us to practically dominate middle-class furnishing in the Manchester locality. No other furnishers in the united Kingdom succesfully conduct in one town two separate establishments of the magnitude of PITTS'S, each of which is greater than any would-be competitors in this city. TvATHAT IS THE NET RESULT 7—Well. judging by the ever-increasing support and the numerous press appreciations and unsolicited testimonials !received, it a quite evident the public generally are thoroughly convinced of PITTS'S ability and desire to please and satisfy them, and that PITTS'S premises, stock, and business are SECOND TO NONE of their kind. But what about the £s. d. net result? you ask. Sorry we cannot enlighten you, as that is just the little bit we reserve for home consumption. THE ORIGIN OF BILE BEANS In all parts of the world, BILE BEANS FOE BlLloneNasa have earned for themselves by their unequalled excellence, a reputation which places them to-day far in advance of any other similar preparation. This specific is not a carelessly prepared nostrum ; on the contrary, it is the result of long and thoughtful research an the part of an Australian Chemist, who after years of study discovered a natural vegetable substance which after farther study he succeeded in compoun ding with other ingredients, so that its effect on the digestive organism of the human system Is identioll with that of the natural bile created by the liver. Find- ag this, then, to be the most cathartic obtainable, the public rapidly accepted it in prefereece to all others, And consequently the old methods of starving and the use of pepsin and bismuth sank into insignificance and oblivion. Acting on the bowels in a gentle manner, Bile Beans do not weaken or in any way distress the Patient. This Is a point in which they differ materially from nearly all laxative remedies sold to-day, as the latter usually act in a violent manner, weakening the patient and In many cases doing more harm than good. Bile beans have been aptly termed "a medicine for the people at a popular price." Many wonder how this remedy can have the great virtue that we claim for it and still be sold at so reasonable a prise ; yet the solu- tion is simple. Millions and millions of Bile Beans are consumed annually (a fact that in itself is of positive proof that the warld's inhabitants know how to appreciate a really genuine remedy) and that they have found that remedy In Charles Forde's Bile Beans for Biliousness.
